# House Plenary: FY 2027 General Appropriations Bill Day 6 Part 1 - September 22, 2026 — Source-Linked Digest

Source: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Nfum87wa8pU

AI-assisted working draft. AI can make errors. Always verify consequential interpretations and wording against the cited transcript and official video.

## At a glance

- **Agency sequence:** The House considered the Office of the Vice President (OVP), Department of Education (DepEd), National Irrigation Administration (NIA), Department of Agriculture (DA), and Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) budgets in that order.

- **Budget scrutiny:** Members asked sponsors about proposed funding, program execution, service gaps, and accountability; requests and sponsor support are distinguished from appropriations.

- **Recording boundary:** Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago's DILG public-market monitoring question continues in Part 2.

Reading note: This digest covers Part 1. Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago's discussion of public-market fees, monitoring and redress continues in Part 2.

## Agency budget proposals

### Principal allocations and comparable requests

The House took up the Office of the Vice President (OVP) budget without a stated request or NEP total. Department of Education (DepEd) sponsor Rep. Maria Carmen S. Zamora described PHP 975.96 billion in the FY2027 National Expenditure Program (NEP) for DepEd and six attached agencies including retirement and life insurance premiums (RLIP); PHP 973.4 billion was for the Office of the Secretary and more than PHP 2.56 billion for the six attached agencies. National Irrigation Administration (NIA) sponsor Rep. Joseph S. Tan gave a FY2027 request of PHP 232.788 billion against a PHP 46.355 billion NEP allocation. Department of Agriculture (DA) sponsor Rep. Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D., described a FY2027 NEP of PHP 198.45 billion for the department, attached agencies, and government corporations, against PHP 215.69 billion appropriated in the FY2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA). Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella described a FY2027 NEP of PHP 330.4 billion including attached agencies. The original Department of Budget and Management (DBM) submission totals for DepEd, DA, and DILG are not stated in this hearing. These are agency allocations and comparisons on the stated bases, not approvals of the appropriations.

### Office of the Vice President budget consideration

The House took up the OVP budget and terminated debate without substantive questioning. The session contains no OVP presentation, request total, or allocation amount. The motion to terminate debate was not an appropriation vote.

[00:17:13 · asr-000109](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000109) · [00:17:54 · asr-000130](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000130) · [00:18:16 · asr-000140](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000140) · [00:18:43 · asr-000154](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000154)

### DepEd program funding, execution, and proposals

DepEd sponsor Rep. Maria Carmen S. Zamora's presentation set out the FY2027 program proposals: PHP 11.4 billion for school-based feeding, PHP 38.12 billion for assistance and subsidies, and PHP 12.09 billion for Expanded Career Progression (ECP). She described PHP 12 billion for the 2027 ECP proposal in a later exchange, against a PHP 500 million average historical allocation; the proposal included a backlog and new promotion applicants, and no additional appropriation was approved. The presentation also covered education and safety programs.

Rep. Antonio L. Tinio cited a PHP 44.45 billion reduction in Office of the Secretary programs from FY2026 GAA to FY2027 NEP. Examples were new school-personnel positions falling from PHP 42.2 billion to PHP 31.7 billion; a PHP 11.4 billion feeding reduction; Indigenous Peoples Education from PHP 154 million to PHP 76 million; computerization from PHP 10.6 billion to PHP 5.7 billion; and textbooks from PHP 19.5 billion to PHP 6.4 billion. Zamora identified PHP 16.03 billion for learning recovery and PHP 50 million for Bayang Bumabasa as DepEd proposals. These were program-level comparisons, not a reconciliation of DepEd's principal total.

Tinio said school-operation allocations would rise 0.11 percent for elementary, 0.38 percent for junior high and 0.33 percent for senior high, against at least 6.2 percent inflation that year and more than 6 percent in 2026. Zamora cited about PHP 30 billion for school maintenance and other operating expenses (MOOE) in 2022 and PHP 61 billion proposed for 2027; Tinio proposed roughly PHP 3 billion more. Zamora agreed inflation had not been sufficiently reflected and undertook to review the formula; no amount was approved.

Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago cited a roughly PHP 14 billion (55.51 percent) feeding reduction; Zamora said coverage would fall from 180 to 120 days and gave about 35.2 million as local-farmer procurement, without restating a currency. Elago sought restoration to the prior level. Other funding questions included PHP 1.97 billion for Last Mile/geographically isolated and disadvantaged area (GIDA) facilities and 131 classrooms; Alternative Learning System (ALS) at PHP 722.13 million in the FY2027 NEP against PHP 897 million in the FY2026 GAA, with Rep. Percival "Perci" Cendaña advocating at least PHP 1 billion and an approximately 622,131-learner target; Asatid monthly compensation of PHP 12,500 and a PHP 16,000 wish-list rate; and PHP 120 million for an AI intervention in DepEd's wish list; Zamora cited a PHP 120 million 2026 allocation for AI-related implementation. This was not specifically an e-care amount, and no continuation or adequacy decision was made. The Asatid increase was a wish-list proposal, subject to government approval. Members asked for details on learner-support reductions and sought restorations; these were proposals and commitments, not appropriation outcomes.

### NIA program mix, costs, utilization, and project accounts

Rep. Joseph S. Tan described the program mix, while Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc and other members raised costs, execution and project details.

Members discussed long-range development estimates separately from the principal request/NEP account. The sponsor cited roughly PHP 8 to 9 billion for restoration and more than PHP 20 billion for new irrigation generation, while a member cited PHP 21 billion for new irrigation. Members also discussed an estimate of PHP 1.6 million per hectare, PHP 960 billion for 600,000 hectares, PHP 640 billion for another 400,000 hectares, PHP 1.6 trillion combined, and PHP 160 billion per year over ten years for new irrigation; these are planning estimates, not appropriation totals, and the hearing gives differing bases. Dagooc separately compared PHP 63.2 billion for FY2026 with PHP 46.3 billion for FY2027; he described these as prior-year and proposed budget figures, but the source does not establish the appropriation basis. This comparison differs from the request/NEP comparison in the lead. NIA reported 49 percent utilization and 28 percent disbursement for 2026, with procurement and other delays. Another round reported a PHP 21 billion cut to national and communal irrigation subsidies and PHP 1.95 billion for small irrigation projects, while the sponsor said about PHP 60 billion of the request would support restoration, improvements and repairs. The rounds identify PHP 100 million for 164 critical watersheds, PHP 100 million for TAGO RIS canal repairs, a PHP 400 million FY2027 Quick Response Fund, and a FY2025 new National Irrigation Systems (NIS) Special Allotment Release Order (SARO) of PHP 14.3 billion with PHP 4.1 billion obligated (28.6 percent). Members requested project and regional breakdowns; Dagooc waived the answer to his request for at least 10,000 hectares of new service area, and NIA debate was terminated.

[03:48:24 · asr-003640](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-003640)

### DA program funding, execution, and proposals

Rep. Antonio L. Tinio, Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago, Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula and other DA interpellators addressed Rice for All, fertilizer support, Rice Competitiveness Enhancement Fund (RCEF), Indigenous Peoples livelihood, fisherfolk insurance, fisheries monitoring, and farm-to-market roads. Rice for All had a PHP 10 billion FY2026 special provision released directly to Food Terminal Incorporated (FTI); the sponsor reported PHP 586.16 million first-half revenue and program results. Bureau of Soils and Water Management (BSWM) Tier 2 had a PHP 536.77 million FY2027 request, with a member saying about PHP 500 million had been denied; the exchange compared 2.4 in the FY2026 GAA with 1.8 proposed for FY2027, without currency or unit, and cited a 34 percent decrease. The Indigenous Peoples 4K livelihood program compared PHP 250 million in FY2026 GAA with PHP 128.29 million in the FY2027 NEP. Weather-index insurance had a PHP 10 million 2025-2026 pilot and a further PHP 10 million FY2027 phase. Vessel Monitoring System/Electronic Reporting System (VMS/ERS) expenditure was PHP 3.440 billion for 2018-2026, with PHP 40 million remaining in June 2026 and PHP 500 million proposed for FY2027.

Bondoc reported a nearly PHP 9 billion request for expanded fertilizer assistance and agreed to provide detailed reporting on fertilizer access. Elago requested details on fuel assistance; Bondoc said fuel support came from DBM savings, would be added to the Quick Response Fund, and that DA was seeking more funds, but gave no 2026 spending total or decision date. Commodity prices, boat costs, tax proposals and private costs remain in the policy exchanges. For RCEF, members discussed a PHP 30 billion tariff-collection threshold, PHP 10 billion set aside in 2025, collections of PHP 34 billion in 2024, PHP 13 billion in 2025 and PHP 4.7 billion in the first quarter of 2026. A PHP 30 billion FY2027 RCEF request was stated, but the modifier before the word request was unclear; Elago asked how the funding shortfall would be addressed, and no mechanism was supplied.

The FY2027 farm-to-market-road (FMR) proposal was PHP 16 billion, with most listed projects at PHP 30 million or less; members examined PHP 500 million and PHP 250 million projects, a PHP 1.54 billion Cordillera Administrative Region total including PHP 1.03 billion for eight Benguet projects, and PHP 2.5 billion for FY2026 Sultan Kudarat roads. Tinio challenged validation and implementability; the sponsor said the projects had been validated and committed to provide supporting records. FMR Watch reported PHP 109.5 billion appropriated for 2021-2026. For FY2025 FMR, PHP 23 billion was allocated and PHP 13 billion released; PHP 10.7 billion under For Later Release (FLR) was described as recently released, while the reported share was disputed. For FY2026, PHP 34.2 billion was appropriated across 1,699 projects, PHP 4.7 billion in SAROs was released, and a PHP 18 billion omnibus SARO awaited release. The rounds distinguish appropriations, releases and awaiting release from completed implementation. A separate 2026 fertilizer augmentation of PHP 2.2 billion had inconsistent scope descriptions; PHP 1.8 billion under FY2027 RCEF concerned biofertilizers, while a different PHP 1.8 billion was discussed for 2025 procurement/utilization. Members requested procurement and bidding detail and reports; no later procurement result was supplied in these exchanges.

Ismula cited separate DA totals of PHP 186 billion FY2026 GAA and PHP 171 billion FY2027 NEP and Production Support Services of PHP 35.6 billion and PHP 29.8 billion, while Bondoc cited a PHP 42 billion FY2027 DBM proposal and PHP 29.7 billion NEP amount for the subprogram. These were differently scoped member comparisons, not reconciled with the sponsor's principal NEP figure.

[07:01:50 · asr-006513](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-006513)

### DILG components, execution, and program funding

DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella described a request for 1,095 emergency-response vehicles, reinstatement of the Seal of Good Local Governance, police and fire-station capacity requests, rescue vehicles and ambulances, and proposed authority for the National Police Commission (NAPOLCOM) to retain self-generated income; these were requests or proposals, not stated funded outcomes. Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula expressed support for increased budgets for the National Commission on Muslim Filipinos (NCMF) and the Philippine Commission on Women (PCW), citing service needs but no amount or adopted increase.

Rep. Presley C. De Jesus separately described a proposed-expenditure program of PHP 310 billion in 2026 and PHP 332 billion in 2027; this is not the principal NEP figure in the lead. The net increase included PHP 21.50 billion attributed to personal services, while capital outlays declined by about PHP 1.97 billion. Fuentebella itemized PHP 4.3 billion for retirement gratuity, PHP 6.4 billion for terminal leave, and PHP 2.091 billion to create 6,000 positions, later giving PHP 2.9 billion for positions; the two position-cost figures were not reconciled. The Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) fee collections were PHP 4.5 billion a year, with DBM funding of PHP 1 billion yearly; Fuentebella sought 80 percent of fee collections for BFP use. De Jesus and Fuentebella discussed equipment and fire-station shortages. Rep. Percival 'Perci' Cendaña asked for a BFP national master plan with annual targets; Fuentebella committed to submit it by the following Monday.

Unified 9-1-1 financial reporting included a 2.45 percent 2025 obligation rate, PHP 1 billion stated for 2025 and about PHP 1.4 billion described as 2025 disbursement while the exchange referred to both 2025 and 2026. Fuentebella committed to provide a same-day spending breakdown. FY2027 9-1-1 funding was PHP 224 million for centers/vehicles; the sponsor later stated a PHP 372 million need and PHP 373 million for operating costs. These are distinct statements, and the ambulance request had no amount.

The Local Government Empowerment Program had PHP 6.494 billion with 69 percent disbursed in 2025. The Local Government Support Fund (LGSF) allocation was PHP 27 billion in 2025, PHP 57 billion in 2026 and PHP 58 billion proposed for 2027; DBM released and selected funds while DILG monitored projects. The Early Childhood Care and Development (ECCD) Council had a PHP 1 billion request, PHP 124 million in the FY2027 NEP and PHP 357 million in the FY2026 GAA; a later PHP 901,716,000 request was not reconciled with the billion-peso amount. Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago cited cuts of PHP 20 million for a child-development-center program and PHP 182 million for accreditation, from PHP 207 million to about PHP 24 million; Fuentebella supported restoration. Elago also cited a 13 percent PCW program cut and called for restoration; no sponsor response or disposition followed in that round.

Gender and Development (GAD) oversight figures were not DILG appropriations: In response to Rep. Iris Marie Demesa Montes, Fuentebella cited PHP 1 trillion in national-entity GAD budgets for FY2025, excluding LGUs. Elago separately cited 80.62 percent utilization in the final GAD report. For the Department of Labor and Employment (DOLE), Elago cited PHP 36 billion in total agency funding and PHP 30.2 billion allocated to GAD (83 percent), as reported in the source's GAD utilization report. The separate measures remain scoped to the named agencies. DILG's original DBM request total was not stated.

## Office of the Vice President (OVP)

### Budget consideration and disposition

The House took up the proposed budget of the Office of the Vice President (OVP) on the motion of Floor Leader Rep. Gil "KabarangayJr" Acosta. Deputy Speaker Jose C. Alvarez was recognized to answer questions, but Rep. Antonio L. Tinio moved to terminate interpellation and debate without substantive questioning. Acosta said the majority joined the motion. Presiding Deputy Speaker Francisco Paolo P. Ortega V approved termination without objection.

[00:17:13 · asr-000109](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000109) · [00:17:54 · asr-000130](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000130) · [00:18:16 · asr-000140](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000140) · [00:18:43 · asr-000154](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-000154)

## Department of Education (DepEd)

### Department of Education (DepEd)

The FY2027 proposal allocates PHP 11.4 billion to DepEd's school-based feeding program. Zamora said it would provide nutritious meals to public-school learners to address malnutrition, improve attendance, and support children's physical and mental readiness to learn.

The proposal allocates PHP 38.12 billion for government assistance and subsidies, which Zamora said would support learners' access to basic education through public and private providers.

The proposal allocates PHP 12.09 billion to the Expanded Career Progression Program to enable the promotion of qualified teachers.

Citing gun violence in schools, Zamora said DepEd's proposed budget provides for school safety and security. She urged the Chamber to give the allocation serious consideration and consider enhancing it at the appropriate time. She gave no amount for the safety allocation or proposed enhancement, and no approval of an increase is recorded in this presentation.

Zamora cited the Philippines' 2025 Programme for International Student Assessment results, describing the country's strongest performance since joining in 2018 and gains from the prior cycle of 20 points in reading, 17 in science, and 16 in mathematics. She said the gains reflected reforms funded in recent years and the work of teachers and learners, and argued that continued investment in learners, teachers, and classrooms would sustain progress.

### Rep. Antonio L. Tinio

Rep. Antonio L. Tinio questioned cuts to major Department of Education (DepEd) programs in the 2027 National Expenditure Program (NEP), comparing it with the 2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA). He cited a PHP 44.45 billion reduction in the Office of the Secretary's programs. Examples included new school personnel positions falling from PHP 42.2 billion in 2026 to PHP 31.7 billion in 2027; the School-Based Feeding Program reduced by PHP 11.4 billion; Indigenous Peoples Education falling from PHP 154 million to PHP 76 million; computerization from PHP 10.6 billion to PHP 5.7 billion; and textbooks and instructional materials from PHP 19.5 billion to PHP 6.4 billion. He also cited reductions in learner support, sports, special needs education, literacy, personnel benefits and other items, and said the Child Protection Program was removed from the 2027 budget. These were Tinio's cited program comparisons, not a full reconciliation of DepEd's total appropriation.

Sponsor Rep. Maria Carmen S. Zamora acknowledged the reductions from the 2026 GAA to the 2027 NEP, while saying DepEd's proposed NEP levels for 2026 and 2027 were almost the same and the House had appropriated more in the 2026 GAA. She said lower funding would affect the Department's performance and left further support to the House. Tinio confirmed that DepEd was relying on Congress for additions.

Asked to state DepEd's priorities, Zamora listed a proposed PHP 16.03 billion for learning recovery, including the Academic Recovery and Accessible Learning (ARAL) Program, and PHP 50 million for the Bayang Bumabasa initiative. She also named additional proposed appropriations for personnel improvement and the learning environment, including textbooks and school safety and security initiatives. Tinio requested the complete priority list; Zamora agreed to provide it, and Tinio said he would obtain it later. No appropriation vote or final disposition on these requested additions appears in this exchange.

Tinio distinguished maintenance and other operating expenses (MOOE) delivered to public schools from DepEd's total MOOE. He said the proposed 2027 school-operation increases were 0.11% for elementary, 0.38% for junior high and 0.33% for senior high, arguing that these were below the inflation figures he cited: at least 6.2% that year and over 6% for 2026. He asked why school-level allocations rose so little.

Zamora said DepEd used a simplified normative formula that considers student and teacher counts, classrooms and the cost of goods and services linked to school standards. She cited about PHP 30 billion for all schools in 2022 and a proposed PHP 61 billion for 2027. Tinio said the formula's calculation did not adequately account for inflation; Zamora agreed inflation was not greatly considered and said DepEd would review the policy and take the observation into account. Tinio said roughly PHP 3 billion more for school MOOE would be needed to keep pace with inflation. This was his estimate and request; no approval or commitment to that amount was recorded.

Tinio asked whether DepEd would request new teaching items for 2027, citing a shortage he put at at least 14,000. Zamora said hiring would continue using 2026 GAA appropriations and available items through December, even without new items in the 2027 proposal. She reported 31,428 positions created under the 2026 GAA, 22,711 filled and 8,688 unfilled as of June 30, 2026, plus 12,646 unfilled positions from prior years; she described the combined total as around 20,000-plus. Asked why hiring lagged despite qualified applicants waiting, she cited hard-to-fill specialized non-teaching roles, special hiring guidelines and shortages of qualified applicants for some specializations. She clarified that the cited 20,000-plus unfilled positions were teaching positions, with other unfilled non-teaching items in addition.

On the Expanded Career Progression (ECP) program, Zamora said the 2027 proposal was PHP 12 billion. Tinio said he understood that about 100,000 of a projected 135,000 promotions represented a backlog, leaving funding for around 35,000 new applicants in 2027; Zamora confirmed that account. She said DepEd had historically averaged PHP 500 million in allocation and promoted about 5,000 teachers, while the current proposal targeted around 135,000 promotions. Looking ahead, she said the Department aimed to promote an average of 100,000 teachers annually, but the 2027 implementation was constrained by the backlog and a more realistic rollout.

Tinio raised concerns from existing licensed guidance counselors, many with master's degrees and salary grade 11, about being overtaken in pay by newly hired school counselor associates. Zamora said DepEd's reclassification request was already with the Department of Budget and Management and gave salary grades 11 to 16 as an example of the proposed adjustment, including incumbent counselors, so they would not be left behind. Tinio said he would follow up with DBM on the salary upgrade. The sponsor reported a pending request, not a completed reclassification.

### Rep. Percival "Perci" Cendaña

Representative Percival 'Perci' Cendaña asked why DepEd issued a follow-up memo about Pride Month attendance after the earlier memo had already described participation as voluntary. DepEd sponsor Representative Maria Carmen S. Zamora said the later memo followed a colleague's request during a committee hearing to emphasize that participation was strictly voluntary, not mandatory. Cendaña said there was nothing wrong with the earlier memo and that LGBT students and pupils deserve to feel accepted in school. He thanked DepEd for embracing inclusion and standing against stigma and discrimination.

Cendaña raised the school shooting in Banga, South Cotabato, which he described as the third such case in three months, and asked what administrative or legal liability DepEd would impose on employees who failed to secure firearms. Zamora said the department was addressing the concern; the parent had already been relieved from his disaster risk reduction and management post at DepEd, and an investigation was pending.

Asked whether DepEd had an internal policy or administrative circular on responsible gun ownership, Zamora said it had no internal policy and left adherence to existing firearm-handling laws to each employee and personnel member. Cendaña urged a prompt circular and possibly stricter gun-ownership policies within the department. Zamora took note and separately said DepEd was committed to improving its implementation of Zones of Peace to make learning environments safer.

Cendaña asked how much of the 2027 Basic Education Facilities Fund (BEFF) and other DepEd programs and activities was earmarked for Last Mile schools and schools in geographically isolated and disadvantaged areas (GIDA), and how many classrooms it would fund. Zamora first cited a 1.9-billion-peso proposal, then specified 1.97 billion pesos under the 2027 NEP and later repeated that amount for the Last Mile School Program. She said it was included in the Basic Education Facilities lump-sum appropriation rather than shown as a separate line item, and that it would fund 131 classrooms.

Zamora said the GIDA Act's implementing rules were still being drafted and were expected by November, the deadline she attributed to the law. Cendaña noted that Last Mile schools, inclusive learning resource centers, technical-vocational laboratories, Alternative Learning System (ALS) centers, and other school buildings were grouped under one line item, making BEFF tracking difficult. He requested a breakdown of BEFF allocations and facility counts by item to make planning more transparent.

Cendaña cited an EDCOM 2 and DepEd estimate of 4.9 million out-of-school youth and asked for the Alternative Learning System (ALS) proposal for 2027. Zamora gave 722.13 million pesos, compared it with 897 million pesos in the 2026 GAA, and characterized the difference as around 75 million pesos, a stated difference that was not reconciled with the two totals. Cendaña described the reduction as more than 70 million pesos.

Asked how many out-of-school youth the proposal could reach, Zamora said it targeted around 622,131 learners for 2027. She compared that with an overall out-of-school-youth figure of around 6 million and said the target remained far short. Cendaña voiced support for increasing ALS funding to at least 1 billion pesos, citing the gap in coverage; this was expressed support, not a recorded appropriation vote.

### Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago

Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago asked the Department of Education (DepEd) to report on its responsibilities under the strengthened early childhood care and development (ECCD) system, including curriculum, staffing and funds for the current and next year. Sponsor Rep. Maria Carmen S. Zamora said ECCD funding was no longer lodged at DepEd but under the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG), with DepEd serving as a co-chair in implementation.

Elago said the ECCD Council had experienced a cut of more than 50 percent and that DILG had reported the available funds would not meet needs for new Child Development Centers. She said responsibility to establish centers now rested with local government units (LGUs), some of which, particularly in 3rd-, 4th- and 5th-class municipalities, lacked capacity and funds. She asked whether DepEd had a line item for monitoring, evaluation and training child development teachers and workers. Zamora said DepEd remained responsible for assisting with ECCD curriculum, while the Council budget was under DILG; she would check how much was proposed for the relevant line item. Elago asked whether DepEd would support a special provision to help LGUs establish centers. Zamora committed DepEd to helping the Council and said its concern extended to all learners, but gave no specific funding commitment. No amount for the ECCD line item or disposition of a special provision was stated in this exchange.

Elago said the 2027 proposal reduced the School-Based Feeding Program by about PHP 14 billion, or 55.51 percent, and asked how the cut would affect service delivery and school coverage. Zamora said the proposed allocation would reduce implementation from 180 days to 120 days and limit universal coverage to kindergarten learners. Elago supported restoring the program's funding to its 2026 level. The exchange records her support for restoration but no vote or final appropriation disposition.

Elago asked whether DepEd procured food directly from local farming and fishing cooperatives and how much of the program's current budget went to them. Zamora said procurement was localized at the division level, with local farmers' rice considered, and cited DepEd data showing around 35.2 million used for local-farmer procurement under the program; the currency was not specified in that answer. Elago requested a detailed report from the Department of Agriculture and raised the need for funding to rescue surplus local produce that might otherwise be discarded and use it in government programs. No response or funding disposition for that proposed food-rescue support appears in the assigned exchange.

Elago said the report she received listed DepEd gender and development activities separately, including the 18-Day Campaign to End Violence Against Women, National Consciousness Day for the Elimination of Violence Against Women, Bawal Bastos Law activities, Committees on Decorum and Investigation, gender-based sexual harassment awareness and Human Rights Month integration. She requested a detailed account of how gender and development was integrated into major programs, including whether community kitchens for school feeding could employ mothers seeking work and support the economic participation of marginalized communities and gender-diverse people. She asked for a utilization report showing integration across programs rather than separate activities. A response to this reporting request is not recorded within this round.

Elago asked how DepEd developed critical thinking, responsible decision-making and civic participation beyond academic performance. Zamora said critical thinking was incorporated into curriculum improvements and instruction, with strategies including expanding electives nationwide, introducing NC3 qualifications for Grade 12 learners, and strengthening life, career, work-immersion and career-aligned elective skills.

Elago then asked what additional resources were needed and cited a 37 percent cut to the Learner Support Program. She requested particulars and warned that inadequate funding could constrain access to counseling and other support services for learners, teachers and education stakeholders, including mental-health services. She also cited uneven support for school libraries, playgrounds, community gardens and spaces for student organizations, and supported higher DepEd funding for school feeding, learner support and mental-health services. Zamora thanked her for supporting the Department's proposed programs but did not provide the requested particulars of the cut or a specific resource commitment in this exchange.

[01:57:06 · asr-001799](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-001799) · [01:59:33 · asr-001825](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-001825)

### Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula

Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula asked sponsor Rep. Maria Carmen S. Zamora to confirm the monthly baseline compensation for Asatids teaching Arabic Language and Islamic Values Education under the Madrasa Education Program. Zamora confirmed PHP 12,500 per month, implemented in 2024. She said DepEd's wish list proposed raising the monthly honorarium from PHP 12,500 to PHP 16,000, subject to Congress; Ismula restated that the increase depended on fund availability and approval, and Zamora agreed. No increase was approved in this exchange. Ismula noted that he had previously raised delayed compensation and commended DepEd for a significant improvement in payment timing, urging that timely release continue. He also said the FY2027 proposed Madrasa Education Program budget was below its FY2026 GAA level and urged restoration at least to that level to ensure support for Asatids and Muslim learners; no amount or disposition of this request was stated here.

On the planned absorption of Islamic Studies and Arabic Language (ISAL) teachers from Sulu, Zamora said around 800 existing teachers would be absorbed automatically without reapplication, subject to further training. She set the effective date at January 1, 2027, and said their compensation would remain under the Bangsamoro Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao (BARMM) through December 2026 before DepEd assumes it in January. Asked for a commitment that current ISAL teachers would be absorbed, Zamora said DepEd was committed and reconfirmed January 1 as the start date. Ismula then raised the lack of omnibus hiring, reappointment, or absorption guidelines and the uncertainty this created for teachers, and urged DepEd to issue guidance promptly. The exchange records the commitment and start date but gives no issuance date for the guidelines.

Ismula asked how DepEd used AI to detect speech impediments and assess learners' health needs through e-care, then asked for e-care's current-year budget and whether it could sustain the projects. Zamora described existing AI interventions, including taking a learner's picture to measure height and weight. She said DepEd's wish list proposed PHP 120 million for AI intervention the next year, subject to government approval. Asked for the 2026 amount, she reported a PHP 120 million allocation for AI-related implementation. She did not identify either figure as an e-care-specific amount or directly address whether the funding was enough to continue the projects.

Passing on questions from an Akbayan colleague, Ismula relayed a teacher organization's concern that teachers had not been adequately consulted about curriculum adjustments, the expanded career progression system, grading changes, and a three-term school calendar, which he said were causing confusion and adding burdens. He asked how DepEd would ensure consultation and corresponding funding before nationwide implementation, and urged it to continue consultations so affected stakeholders were reached, with proper orientation, teaching funds, and rest for teachers before calendar or curriculum changes. Zamora said several consultations and policy orientations were already under way and committed DepEd to reaching stakeholders so policies and program improvements would be informed and communicated. Her answer addressed consultation and orientations but did not address the question's corresponding-funding point.

Asked about burnout and demoralization from repeated, sudden policy changes, Zamora described DepEd teacher-wellness interventions and cited a two-day wellness break announced the previous week. Ismula welcomed the break, noting that his sister, a DepEd teacher, had used it, and urged the department to continue it. In closing, he said teachers provide both instruction and care to children and urged colleagues not to withhold needed funding and rest from teachers and personnel.

[02:11:50 · asr-001928](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-001928) · [02:12:10 · asr-001930](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-001930)

### Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc

Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc relayed the longstanding appeal from Dr. Allan Laniba of Karate-do Pilipinas Region 8 and Filipino Shotokan Karatedo (Filshokai) to include Karate-do in Palarong Pambansa. He noted its presence in Batang Pinoy but continued exclusion from the Palarong Pambansa roster, and asked whether it could be reconsidered. DepEd sponsor Representative Maria Carmen S. Zamora said the matter would be placed on the relevant board's agenda for its next meeting, where consideration of Karate-do as a demonstration sport would be taken up. Dagooc said he would provide Laniba's letter later.

Dagooc asked what DepEd reports under the budget special provision requiring quarterly financial and physical accomplishment reports through the Unified Reporting System within 30 days each quarter. Zamora said the Department of Education (DepEd) submits quarterly physical and financial accomplishments to the Department of Budget and Management (DBM), as all agencies must. She said the report covers the department's budget and fund utilization alongside progress against physical targets.

Dagooc asked whether the reports track completed and unfinished classroom construction and the number of teaching and non-teaching positions filled against annual allocations. Zamora confirmed both are included.

Dagooc returned to the school feeding program, asking whether its allocation had been moved into another budget item after the standalone line disappeared. Zamora distinguished the comparison bases: the 2027 NEP shows a decrease against the 2026 GAA, while the 2027 NEP is almost at the same level as DepEd's proposed 2026 NEP. No amounts were stated in this exchange.

Dagooc described the program's importance to children who arrive at school without breakfast and cited the Siargao and Dinagat electric cooperatives, saying that two or three schools had been adopted and received regular feeding. Zamora called feeding a departmental priority and, while acknowledging budget limits, said the department wished for the program's proposed allocation to increase under the 2027 GAA. This was a departmental wish, not a recorded appropriation motion.

Asked about DepEd Order No. 40, Series of 2012, Zamora identified the Child Protection Policy, including school Child Protection Committees and other interventions such as the Learner Rights Protection Specialization course. Dagooc asked whether every school had a committee. He noted that not all did and referred to a count of 33,000, which he said had grown substantially; Zamora said the policy mandates a committee in every school but current data showed 33,000 functional committees.

Dagooc pressed on whether committees were functioning, citing recent incidents and his concern for his grandchildren in school. Zamora committed that DepEd would intensify implementation of the program, especially creating Child Protection Committees in schools.

Dagooc moved to terminate interpellation and debate on the Department of Education and its attached agencies for fiscal year 2027. Floor Leader Rep. Alyssa Michaela "Mica" M. Gonzales said the majority joined. With no objection heard, presiding Deputy Speaker Francisco Paolo P. Ortega V announced that the DepEd budget was approved. The motion raised in this exchange was to end debate; the recorded disposition here is procedural termination, not a separate appropriation motion.

## National Irrigation Administration (NIA)

### National Irrigation Administration (NIA)

Rep. Joseph S. Tan, presenting the proposed fiscal year 2027 budget of the National Irrigation Administration (NIA), described irrigation as support for farm production, farmers' livelihoods and food security. He argued that NIA should expand irrigation to rain-dependent agricultural areas, rehabilitate aging or damaged facilities, modernize water delivery, and build infrastructure able to withstand droughts, floods, typhoons and climate change.

Tan said funding should translate into hectares irrigated, reliable water, higher farm productivity and incomes, with projects planned for readiness and completed on time. He called for stronger partnerships with irrigators' associations, farmers and local governments, and said irrigation should complement seeds, fertilizer, machinery, post-harvest facilities, farm-to-market roads, credit and market access. He emphasized that these investments would be less effective where farms still lacked water.

Tan also called on Congress to demand accountability for delays and measurable results, including farmers helped, hectares receiving reliable irrigation, additional harvests and improved farmer incomes. He submitted NIA's proposed FY2027 budget for the chamber's consideration and said he was ready to answer questions. This presentation states neither NIA's principal FY2027 NEP allocation nor its original agency request, and gives no amount from which either can be inferred. Submission for consideration is not an appropriation approval.

[02:35:40 · asr-002240-split-3](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-002240-split-3) · [02:37:00 · asr-002243](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-002243) · [02:37:30 · asr-002244](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-002244) · [02:39:19 · asr-002278](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-002278)

### Rep. Arlene "Kaka" J. Bag-ao

Rep. Arlene "Kaka" J. Bag-ao thanked the National Irrigation Administration (NIA) for data it had provided after an earlier committee hearing, then asked how it would close the irrigation gap. Citing NIA's figures, she said 70.25 percent, or 2.20 million hectares, were irrigated and 1.01 million hectares remained; average annual coverage growth over 15 years was 1.14 percent. For 2027, NIA's target was 5,362 hectares of new irrigation nationwide, which she rounded to about 5,000 in questioning. She asked for the timeline and strategy to reach full coverage. Sponsor Rep. Joseph S. Tan said the National Irrigation Master Plan identifies more than 9 million hectares of net potential irrigable land, while NIA's working baseline from the prior year was 3.2 million hectares, of which about 2.2 million were irrigated. He attributed the long path partly to conversion of farmland and said about 1 million hectares remained under that baseline. Bag-ao challenged treating land conversion as a reason to accept low targets and urged quicker irrigation of prime farmland. Tan suggested local small-water-impounding projects or man-made lagoons sized to the area served as complementary interventions.

Tan described the remaining development need as a long-term national funding challenge. He first gave a PHP 640 billion figure while discussing 600,000 hectares to be irrigated by 2030; he later calculated 600,000 hectares at about PHP 1.6 million per hectare, or PHP 960 billion. For 400,000 hectares beyond 2030 he later gave PHP 640 billion, together describing the two stages as PHP 1.6 trillion, or PHP 160 billion per year over ten years for new irrigation development alone. He said that estimate excluded restoration, rehabilitation, and repairs. In the earlier estimate he also referred to "another 400 something" for the 400,000 hectares without restating the unit; the transcript does not reconcile that phrase or the initial PHP 640 billion figure with his later calculations. These are broad development-cost estimates, not NIA's FY2027 request or NEP allocation.

During discussion of strategic coverage, Tan said the NEP covered about 19 percent of NIA's request. He described rough portions of the allocation: PHP 8-9 billion for restoration and more than PHP 20 billion for new irrigation generation, not a complete reconciliation of the NEP total. Bag-ao later cited PHP 21 billion for NIA new irrigation and a separate, unquantified Department of Agriculture allocation; these were differently rounded accounts of new-irrigation funding. She urged colleagues to increase funding for irrigation and make it a priority in any budget adjustment, given the remaining coverage gap and risk of agricultural-land conversion. Her appeal remained a call for prioritization.

Bag-ao asked whether NIA should lead or coordinate irrigation work assigned across agencies, including the Department of Agriculture (DA), to avoid divided targets and added costs. Tan said NIA and regional irrigation master plans involve DA and other agencies and are intended to prevent duplication; he noted that NIA, DA, and the Department of Agrarian Reform (DAR) have connected responsibilities around land and hoped their roles would be clarified further, including work on national and communal irrigation systems. Bag-ao also asked NIA's view of establishing a Department of Rural Development to focus rural strategy, particularly in remote areas. Tan called the proposal a good suggestion, said the agency had heard it, and pointed to the Regional Irrigation Master Plan and noted the proposal. Bag-ao emphasized that such a department could affect institutional responsibilities, workforce division, and targets.

Bag-ao asked for a breakdown of the 2027 target showing how many of the roughly 5,000 hectares would be in Mindanao, a detailed master-plan work plan addressing jurisdictional conflicts and target allocation, and a serious review of NIA's budget to add irrigation targets for more land, especially prime agricultural land at risk of conversion. She also called for a strategic timeline and interventions, and said irrigation should be prioritized in any budget adjustment. Tan said NIA evaluates ongoing and proposed projects and that legislators could help guide attention to farms needing irrigation; he did not state Mindanao's share or a concrete timeline. Bag-ao requested data for stakeholder feedback and monitoring, then thanked someone who handed her a document, but the transcript does not identify its contents. In closing she reiterated the budget review and Mindanao coverage questions; Tan again said projects were being evaluated and that he would help guide irrigation to farms.

### Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago

Representative Sarah Jane I. Elago cited National Irrigation Administration (NIA) briefing figures of 4.2 million hectares of potentially irrigable land and 2.2 million hectares developed, leaving 2 million hectares to develop. She asked why nearly 1 million potentially irrigable hectares remained outside the system and what the work would cost. NIA sponsor Representative Joseph S. Tan said the regional irrigation master plan placed net potential above 4 million hectares; he contrasted that with older data of 3.2 million hectares and said that, against 2.2 million developed, the remaining balance was about 1 million hectares. He attributed the gap to limited funding.

Tan estimated the conversion cost at PHP 1.6 million per hectare and said developing the remaining area would cost almost PHP 1 trillion. Spread across 10 years, he estimated PHP 160 billion per year for conversion alone, excluding restoration, improvements, and repairs. The cost estimates were not reconciled arithmetically in the exchange. Elago recalled Tan's 2024 estimate of PHP 200 billion per year for the country's irrigation needs and said current funding for NIA was below 25 percent of the required annual amount.

Asked how much of the existing service area was productive and actively cultivated, how much was underused for lack of water, and how much was covered by small irrigation projects, Tan said the earlier 2.2-million-hectare figure came from old potential-area data. He described housing, industrial, and other non-agricultural conversions as reducing the irrigable base, said nearly 50,000 hectares had been converted, and put the most reliable current irrigated area at nearly 1.5 million hectares.

Elago called for tighter land-use conversion rules to protect productive and prime agricultural land, livelihoods, and food and rice self-sufficiency. Tan said conversion clearances involve the Department of Agrarian Reform (DAR), Department of Agriculture (DA), NIA, and local government units (LGUs), with the Department of Human Settlements and Urban Development (DHSUD) also signing off on individual land-titling and site-development plans. He identified LGUs as especially important because they know local areas and planning, and urged the agencies to coordinate and enforce the law to prevent indiscriminate conversion.

Elago said the FY 2027 budget message named agriculture as a priority but cited cuts to irrigation support: a PHP 21 billion cut to subsidies for national and communal irrigation systems and a PHP 1.95 billion cut to small irrigation projects. She also said agriculture's contribution to GDP was at a historic low of 1.8 percent.

Tan said NIA's request covered developing irrigable land and restoration, improvements, and repairs. He said limited fiscal space required funds to be spread among agencies, leaving large, multi-year irrigation projects fragmented and delayed. Elago asked how much of NIA's original proposal was for communal systems and small irrigation projects. Tan estimated that, within the original proposal, about PHP 60 billion would cover restoration, improvements, and repairs, with the rest for new irrigation; he did not provide separate totals for the two project categories. Elago requested the breakdown of the original proposal and voiced support for increasing funding for communal systems and small irrigation projects.

[03:21:56 · asr-003081](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-003081)

### Rep. Percival "Perci" Cendaña

Rep. Percival "Perci" Cendaña asked how much the National Irrigation Administration (NIA) proposed 2027 budget had increased over 2026. Sponsor Rep. Joseph S. Tan did not give a year-to-year increase. He described the NEP plus local-source funding as almost PHP 47 billion, a different basis from the principal NEP allocation. No matched 2026 total was supplied for the year-to-year question.

Cendaña asked about use of the 2026 budget. Tan reported a utilization rate of about 49 percent and a disbursement rate of 28 percent. He attributed the gap to the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) release arriving around midyear, followed by procurement and post-evaluation that he estimated took about two months and three months overall. He said delays then pushed work into the rainy season, when weather and site conditions further constrained construction. He expected NIA to accelerate procurement and implementation to raise obligations, physical accomplishment and disbursement.

Cendaña asked how many irrigation projects were delayed. Tan said NIA had a count and would provide it, citing rain and muddy sites, fuel increases, scarce construction materials, equipment that could not be mobilized, and climate change among the causes. Cendaña asked for counts of completed projects and those completed but not operational. Tan said current-year procurement and mobilization were still underway and NIA's work could take a year; he agreed to send the lists of completed, delayed and non-operational projects.

Tan said some systems that once delivered water had become non-operational because climate change or storms affected supply, or seepage prevented water from reaching the end of a system. Repair, rehabilitation or restoration could return them to use. He said NIA's funds were divided between developing new irrigable land and making existing systems operational again, and that additional funding could support more repairs, rehabilitation and restoration. Cendaña said he expected substantial delays and planned to seek a computation of the income farmers lost because of administrative and governance gaps; he said he would make that a motion at the end of the interpellation. No delayed-project count, completion list or farmer-income estimate is provided in this exchange, and no motion disposition is recorded here.

Cendaña asked how NIA was preparing for El Niño and water-security risks, including drought-resistant irrigation planning, reservoir storage, canal rehabilitation, alternative systems and efficient water allocation. Tan described short-term measures: maintain irrigation facilities, consider crops with shorter growing periods, optimize water use, use impounding areas or man-made lagoons with solar pumps, build small reservoir irrigation facilities, and study groundwater augmentation and artificial aquifer recharge. He said recharge would need broader study. He also described watershed and water-management efforts, public information about conserving and collecting rainwater, and encouraging schools to install cisterns.

For longer-term work, Tan cited improvements to national and communal irrigation systems, dams and facilities, stronger water management, expanded water resources and renewable energy, watershed protection, ecosystem restoration, capacity building and stakeholder partnerships. He said most of NIA's funding was aligned with El Niño preparations. Asked for assurance that funds would be released on time, Tan said the full NAA had come out around midyear and NIA was accelerating procurement and disbursement to maximize resources and increase physical accomplishment; he gave no amount or quantified deadline.

Cendaña said NIA's potential irrigable-area assessment was approximately 4.65 million hectares, compared with a long-used 3.13 million hectares, and asked what the revised area meant for project costs. Tan said the larger figure included land with steeper slopes, citing 3-to-8-percent, 8-to-15-percent and 45-percent slopes, while describing 3 percent as the usual slope basis. He said additional funding would be important to develop more irrigable land but did not calculate a cost impact. Later, Tan referred to a 4.250 figure, a 4-million-plus potential, and a 3.2-million-hectare baseline based on a 3-percent slope; Cendaña characterized the difference as roughly one million. The exchange does not reconcile these varying area figures, so they remain attributed to the speakers.

Cendaña also asked how NIA coordinated irrigation planning with other agencies, including the Bureau of Soils and Water Management (BSWM), which he said was operating in silos with NIA. Tan said NIA and agencies including the Department of Agriculture (DA) and BSWM, the Philippine Institute of Volcanology and Seismology (PHIVOLCS), Department of Agrarian Reform (DAR), and National Commission on Indigenous Peoples (NCIP) were continuously studying irrigation issues. He did not describe a specific coordination mechanism. Cendaña reiterated the need for a whole-of-government response to El Niño and thanked the sponsor.

### Rep. Elijah Rumbaoa San Fernando

Rep. Elijah Rumbaoa San Fernando said he supported irrigation projects but wanted accountability for the National Irrigation Administration's (NIA) mandate. He cited a PHP 46.36 billion requested NIA budget for FY2027 and asked whether NIA would publish searchable, project-level information, including contract identifiers, geotagged locations, contractors, budgets and implementation status. Sponsor Rep. Joseph S. Tan said NIA was building a transparency portal to make irrigation-project information accessible. When asked for a release date, Tan hoped to have it by year-end and said the latest might be the first quarter, given two or three months of procurement; he said NIA would try to finish it. San Fernando treated year-end or the first quarter as the commitment, but Tan framed the timing as a hope, not a firm dated commitment.

San Fernando also sought supporting records for each project, including BP Form 202, Program of Works, geotagged photos and coordinates, a Certificate of Implementability, and local certificates of no objection or support. Tan agreed such records would help protect public funds, avoid overlap and show where projects would be placed, and said NIA was doing this work. San Fernando said on the record that he expected those supporting documents and later said he would revisit the issue until NIA addressed project transparency. After the exchange, Tan added that NIA already had a website and would integrate the requested information; he gave no separate timeline for that integration.

San Fernando asked whether NIA's master plan and feasibility studies set irrigable-area estimates, annual targets and project locations, and referred to a 30 percent irrigation target. Tan said the 30 percent referred to remaining land to be developed. He cited a 3.2 million-hectare land area, with 70 percent developed and about 1 million hectares, or 30 percent, still being filled in as eligible land. He estimated that covering the 1 million hectares would cost more than PHP 1 trillion; he separately described about PHP 640 billion for 600,000 hectares and more than PHP 400 billion for another 400,000 by 2030, or around PHP 160 billion per year over ten years, solely for development. Tan's approximate totals and annual estimate were not reconciled arithmetically in the exchange.

San Fernando cited a 1.5 percent annual NIA accomplishment rate. Tan said the 1.5 percent was an increase shown over the ten years from 2015 and referred to 4 percent as the area irrigated; the exchange does not fully clarify the denominator for those percentages. Tan affirmed that the master plan and feasibility studies guided where and what projects would be built. He described a National Irrigation Master Plan and Regional Irrigation Master Plans to identify potential areas and set a pattern for yearly work. For smaller pump projects, he said irrigation could cost more where few hectares or steep slopes were involved, while a pump could serve remote locations with a 3 percent slope.

San Fernando challenged the specificity of the National Expenditure Program (NEP) line items despite NIA's stated use of master plans and feasibility studies. He cited PHP 100 million in the General Appropriations Bill for rehabilitation and protection of watershed management and asked where it would be spent. Tan said the amount covered 164 critical watersheds nationwide; he said NIA had a list and agreed to provide it, but described placement only generally by suitable area. San Fernando pressed why the locations were not included in the proposal.

He also cited a PHP 100 million TAGO RIS repair-of-irrigation-canal line and examples such as concrete pavement, a service road at Santa Maria RIS and intake improvement at Alip RIS. Tan agreed that projects should be enumerated. He said NIA's system contained more particular details, but barangay and municipality details had not appeared in the NEP and would be made specific before the FY2027 General Appropriations Act. San Fernando rejected a clerical-error explanation, said his random checks found many general or undetailed entries, and maintained that the answer did not resolve the lack of project detail. Tan's response was a promise to add specificity before enactment, while the member maintained his objection.

San Fernando asked what NIA's artificial intelligence (AI) monitoring for solar-pump irrigation projects covered, which indicators and data it used, how the data informed decisions, and whether the system would be fully disclosed. Tan first asked which system he meant. After San Fernando clarified that he meant AI monitoring generally, Tan said it monitored pump performance and could inform crop health and cropping calendars, including whether rain was expected to help farmers using solar pumps. San Fernando requested a document explaining the system so he could study it. No specific project list, indicators beyond those functions, data disclosure details or delivery date for the requested document was provided.

[04:11:22 · asr-003892](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-003892) · [04:11:46 · asr-003896](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-003896)

### Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc

Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc compared the National Irrigation Administration (NIA) budget he cited as PHP 63.2 billion for FY 2026 with PHP 46.3 billion proposed for FY 2027, calling the reduction 26%. Sponsor Rep. Joseph S. Tan answered with the request-to-NEP comparison and described the difference as 19.9%. Tan's request-to-NEP comparison was distinct from Dagooc's prior-year budget comparison, although Tan called 19.9% the difference without explaining the calculation.

Asked which programs accounted for the reduction, Tan said restoration, improvements, and rehabilitation represented about 30 to 35% relative to new irrigation development. He could not itemize all affected projects in the time available but offered to provide the affected or unfunded locations. He described restoration as repairing or rebuilding canals and facilities damaged by calamities, typhoons, displacement, or erosion; the work varies by project and can overlap with repairs and improvements. Dagooc asked whether the program also covered previously reported problem areas. Tan said those areas were included, with work phased over time and restoration funds usable across National Irrigation Systems and Communal Irrigation Systems.

Dagooc asked whether NIA's FY 2027 proposal included a drought-resilience program in light of the forecast through the first quarter of 2027. Tan cited a Quick Response Fund of approximately PHP 400 million as his response to the drought-funding question. Dagooc described a solar-powered pump that still could not supply enough water for all the land, leading owners to stop planting, and asked whether NIA had a response to drought-related water shortages.

Tan said the location could be studied with NIA Administrator Guillen and recognized that pumping from a well may not be sustainable when drought depletes the water. As interim options, he suggested that farmers consider short-term high-value crops needing less water, or count the farmers and land served in a summer cycle to size a man-made water-storage lagoon that could be filled and pumped with solar power. These were suggestions for follow-up and alternatives, not a stated funded project commitment.

Dagooc cited a FY 2025 New National Irrigation System subprogram Special Allotment Release Order (SARO) of PHP 14.3 billion, of which PHP 4.1 billion had been obligated, a 28.6% utilization rate, and asked what prevented use of the funds. Tan said funds may remain unobligated while projects are procured or await award and notice to proceed. For large projects requiring foreign partners, he described repeated bidding problems and a lack of qualified contractors, which can delay obligations even after funds arrive. Dagooc agreed with the need for irrigation funding but stressed that unused funds were a concern.

On performance, Dagooc said the 2025 service-area target was 8,692 hectares against 3,162 hectares accomplished. He said the FY 2027 budget was lower while the target rose from 8,620 to 11,153, and asked Administrator Guillen to focus on reaching at least 10,000; he cited a pattern of achieving about half of targets. Dagooc expressly waived a further answer on that appeal. Dagooc then moved to terminate debate on NIA's FY 2027 proposed budget. The Majority joined the motion; after the Deputy Speaker heard no objection, the Chair declared interpellations terminated.

## Department of Agriculture (DA)

### Department of Agriculture (DA)

Bondoc pointed to 3.1% growth in the agriculture, forestry and fishing sector in 2025, reversing a 1.5% contraction in 2024. She said second-quarter agriculture and fisheries production value rose 2.9% year on year to PHP 452.22 billion at constant 2018 prices, with increases reported across crops, livestock, poultry and fisheries. She cited Rice for All, 20 Pesos Rice, Kadiwa, enhanced buffer stocking and support under the Rice Tariffication Law as efforts to keep food accessible while supporting producers.

She cautioned that higher production value does not itself secure physical production, and framed the challenge as keeping food affordable while farming and fishing remain profitable. Her answer was a value-chain approach rather than reliance on one commodity, agency or program. She said the FY2027 budget should protect gains and invest in productivity and lower costs, farm and fishery infrastructure, post-harvest loss reduction, cold chains and logistics, market access, irrigation, fisheries modernization, animal health and biosecurity, anti-smuggling work, climate resilience and disaster preparation. She added that DA responsibilities had not fallen with the smaller proposed budget and described the department's priorities as also including technology, adaptation and mitigation, preparedness, and stronger value chains.

Bondoc urged the House to measure appropriations by results: more productive land, resilient fishing families, schooling for farmers' and fishers' children, roads linking farms to markets, reduced crop losses, animal protection, enforcement against smugglers, disaster protection and access to safe, nutritious food at reasonable prices. She called for a forward-looking FY2027 investment in productivity, profitability, resilience and food security that addresses agricultural poverty and attracts younger people, with farmers and fishers as competitive participants and self-investors. She said the intended results were better harvests, higher incomes, lower losses, stronger rural communities and more profitable farmers, and urged support for the DA and its attached agencies and corporations.

### Rep. Renee Louise M. Co

Before turning to the FY 2027 budget, Representative Renee Louise M. Co asked why promised reports had not reached the committee, including a Pax Silica impact investigation and a Bureau of Animal Industry anti-rabies report. After Co corrected the record that the anti-rabies report had arrived, she asked for the remaining Pax Silica report, which the Gabriela Women's Party representative had also requested. Agriculture sponsor Representative Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D. said the Department of National Defense had referred her inquiry to the Bases Conversion and Development Authority (BCDA) and argued that food security required multiple agencies to work together. She said the report was comprehensive and that the deadline given to them was the end of September.

Co asked whether the report would cover farmers' land claims at the site and whether the Department of Agriculture (DA) would collaborate with the BCDA. Bondoc said the impact assessment also needed to address power and water demands and farmers within a wider radius; she said DA would be one of the lead agencies preparing a position paper and impact assessment with other agencies, including the Department of National Defense. She said DA would complete its portion by the end of September. Co requested a copy once available.

Co cited the Magna Carta for Agricultural Workers and Republic Act 8435 in discussing the government's duty to protect prime agricultural land. She said DA had promised geofencing guidelines to prevent solar infrastructure from encroaching on 3 million hectares of prime agricultural land and asked about their legal status. Bondoc said, after conferring with the secretary, geofencing would be completed by the end of December.

Asked why the guidelines would take until then, Bondoc said DA needed validated data. Some prime agricultural lands remained underwater after major flooding, preventing validation until conditions settled; she said validation would proceed as soon as possible afterward.

Co asked how DA would keep remaining farms within agricultural classification and whether farmers' insecure land tenure undermined agricultural productivity and food security. She cited the briefing's average farm-size figure as "3.59 to 2.03 hectares," whose relationship was unclear. Bondoc said preserving agricultural land was a major concern and described a two-part approach: make farming more productive and profitable through lower post-harvest losses, irrigation, fertilizer and pesticide support, and better farm-gate prices so farmers are less pressured to sell; and work with local government units on strict conversion rules and, if necessary, conversion moratoriums to stop developers from converting and damaging irrigated land.

Co linked low farm profitability and land insecurity with a shrinking and aging agricultural workforce. Citing prior-hearing figures, she said the sector had 10.44 million registered workers, about 20% of the national workforce but 7.9% of GDP, and an average age of 56, with low youth participation. She questioned why direct, non-collateral production subsidies were often limited or hard to access while loans were more available and could deepen farmers' debt. Bondoc answered that DA continued direct assistance, including seeds through rice support programs, fertilizer, diesel, and marketing support. She said credit could also help farmers and cooperatives mature into financially stable enterprises; DA trains and supports less mature cooperatives and irrigators' associations with mechanization, seeds, and fertilizer so they can eventually operate profitably and take loans. Co emphasized that farmers can be excluded from direct-subsidy lists, while loan defaults and repayment burdens may discourage farmers, young people, and heirs from staying in agriculture.

Asked how to draw people into farming when many do not own the land they cultivate and direct support is limited, Bondoc said profitability was decisive: low farm-gate prices and poor market access deter entrants, while irrigation could increase cropping from one to three seasons in her cited Upper Pampanga River Irrigation System example. She also argued that farming should be treated as honorable and a first-choice livelihood, including for people under 30. Co agreed with the goal but argued that farmers struggle to survive against imports and policies she said favor import access and lower-priced imports over domestic production; she called for changes to rice and other agricultural import dependence and genuine agrarian land reform, with enforcement capacity for DA and the Department of Agrarian Reform.

[05:04:31 · asr-004404](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-004404)

### Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc

Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc asked why PHP 10 billion appropriated for the program was directed in a lump sum to Food Terminal Incorporated (FTI). Department of Agriculture (DA) sponsor Rep. Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D. said PHP 10 billion had been released directly to FTI for the program's expansion in 2026 and that FTI, the DA's implementation partner, pays for the rice. Bondoc reported that in the first half of 2026 the program reached 33.2% of its annual operational target and generated PHP 586.16 million in total revenue against funds allocated for procurement and distribution.

Dagooc asked how the Registry System for Basic Sectors in Agriculture (RSBSA) supports DA programs and raised reported gaps in 2025 validation activities to update farmer and farm-parcel information in nine regions. Bondoc confirmed the registry is used to determine eligibility for seeds, fertilizer, and fuel assistance, but acknowledged that it undercounts farmers: she estimated about 40% are not captured. Registration depends heavily on local government units (LGUs) and municipal agricultural offices or workers, while DA has limited direct reach below the regional level because agricultural services were devolved. Dagooc said farmers, including members of his own family, had complained of missing assistance and asked what was being corrected. Bondoc agreed that unregistered farmers can be left out when aid is distributed; farmers may register at a municipality, but many do not travel there until after assistance has gone out. She said more field staff are needed to reach farmers.

Asked for concrete corrective steps, Bondoc said DA staff reach only the regions and identified a need for 23,000 extension workers down to barangay and purok levels. DA is conducting information campaigns and encouraging self-registration, while also saying it cannot rely on farmers to come to the agency and must reach them. Dagooc asked DA to use municipal and city agriculturists. Bondoc agreed that DA and LGUs should cooperate, and said online self-registration and an RSBSA LGU app were planned for the last quarter of 2026 so local officials could check their lists against the registry. She also cited focused registration drives and coordination with DA programs, attached agencies, and other government institutions. Dagooc requested an update to Congress through the Committee on Agriculture in the first quarter of 2027. Bondoc agreed to provide updates and inform him when the online registration and LGU app became available.

Dagooc questioned why DA routed the Rice for All funds through a corporate entity instead of managing them through its regular regulated channels, and asked whether DA accepted the arrangement and what safeguards protected the funds. Bondoc answered that the National Food Authority (NFA) has no mandate to sell rice, which is why implementation goes through FTI. She said DA had not encountered major problems with the arrangement so far; it was only in its first year, with eight months going well. She was open to discussing additional safeguards but did not identify specific safeguards in this exchange.

[05:21:04 · asr-004647](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-004647)

### Rep. Iris Marie Demesa Montes

Representative Iris Marie Demesa Montes asked what FY 2027 budget strategies would reduce import dependence and produce measurable self-sufficiency targets. Department of Agriculture (DA) sponsor Representative Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D. said imports were intended to cover gaps in local production and described rice self-sufficiency as 70%. She then said stocks consisted of 12% local production and 62% imports; the exchange did not reconcile these figures or identify the stock-share denominator.

Bondoc pointed to the National Rice Program and Rice Competitiveness Enhancement Fund (RCEF), which she linked to the Rice Tariffication Law, as vehicles for seeds, infrastructure, training, fertilizer, pesticides, marketing support, farm-to-market roads, and irrigation. She said DA wanted to reduce import needs, advocated higher import tariffs, and emphasized supporting local farmers so they could raise production. Citing volatility and conflict-related disruptions in world markets, she warned that imports might not be available even when the country had money to buy. The response described programs and policy positions but did not identify a specific FY 2027 line item or quantified self-sufficiency target.

Asked how much fertilizer and feed supply came from local production versus imports, Bondoc answered with DA fertilizer distribution figures: it supplied registered farmers and could reach 2 out of 10, while she said coverage remained short. She referred to a prior discussion of 4 bags of fertilizer per hectare, then later stated that the requirement was 10 bags per hectare and DA was giving 2 bags per farmer. The response gives assistance coverage and bag quantities, not a local-versus-import share.

Asked how DA would sustain productivity beyond short-term subsidies and inputs, Bondoc argued that DA funding should increase and that reducing support by 8% could reverse gains. She said support should help farmer organizations become trained, modernized, and financially mature, so assistance could later reach other groups; she also characterized the Rice Tariffication Law as a control on imports and said DA was pursuing amendments to support local farmers and reduce imports.

As evidence of progress, Bondoc said rice self-sufficiency was around 77% and 78% in 2023 and 2022, fell to 74% in 2024, and had returned to 78%. She described palay production as trending upward from 2017 to 2025, with a decline during the 2024 El Nino and a rise again in 2025. She also said the second quarter had reached its highest level in 40 years, while the 2026 DA budget was still being rolled out and gains were not yet fully visible. She used these trends to argue for maintaining and increasing budget support.

Montes asked for measures showing whether DA programs improve farmers' incomes, livelihoods, and jobs, including an independent third-party audit. Bondoc said she could assess progress through statistics and individual accounts. She cited falling poverty incidence among farmers and fisherfolk, attributing the official figures to the Philippine Statistics Authority, DA, and other agencies, and said she also used local visits and farmers' stories.

Bondoc cited a farmer who thanked her after receiving PHP 2,325 and said he now had two civil-engineer children; she used the anecdote to illustrate changes in farmers' household opportunities, without specifying the payment program or basis. She also reported that farmer income had risen from 10,710 to 14,620 and gave palay price figures of 11 last year, 9 as another reported figure, 15 reported to her that day, and 23 in the official material. The exchange does not state the unit or period for the income figures or a currency and quantity unit for the price figures. Bondoc did not identify an independent third-party audit; she cited agency statistics and personal accounts instead.
