### Rep. Presley C. De Jesus

Rep. Presley C. De Jesus said the Bureau of Soils and Water Management (BSWM) requested PHP 536.77 million for FY2027 under Tier 2 to expand national soil infrastructure. He asked how much the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) denied after the Department of Agriculture (DA) cut. Sponsor Rep. Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D., said about PHP 500 million was denied from BSWM, but did not reconcile that figure with the request or specify the remaining amount included in the proposal.

Bondoc compared BSWM figures of 2.4 in the 2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA) and 1.8 for FY2027, and called this a 34% decrease. She did not state a currency or unit with those two figures in this exchange. De Jesus asked how many of the 11 provincial soil laboratories he said were targeted for 2027 had been defunded and which provinces were affected. Bondoc said the 2026 targets would proceed, but 16 soil laboratories could not be funded for 2027; she did not give a province list. The exchange does not reconcile the 11-lab target in the question with the 16 unfunded laboratories in the response.

De Jesus then asked whether underfunding the 2027 laboratories made BSWM's target of 30 functional soil-testing facilities by 2028 unattainable. Bondoc agreed that targets may need adjustment to match funded projects. Asked for a revised timeline, she gave no date: she said the 16 unfunded laboratories would be requested again for 2028, hoped fiscal space would be found, and said House and Senate budget negotiations were ongoing and the funding might still increase.

De Jesus asked about former employees of the Sugar Regulatory Administration (SRA) and its early-retirement incentive program (ERIP), including whether the controversy followed the 2015 rationalization plan and whether benefits were released after employees separated on August 1, 2016. Bondoc said the Supreme Court had ruled for payment but that the Governance Commission for Government-Owned or Controlled Corporations (GCG) would determine which employees were entitled. She repeated that the Department of Agriculture (DA) respected the ruling and was waiting for GCG to identify eligible employees.

De Jesus asked whether all affected names had been sent to GCG. Bondoc confirmed that the list of concerned employees had been submitted and said the DA would follow up. De Jesus then cited the Court's ruling in Villanueva Jr. v. Sugar Regulatory Administration involving 75 former officials and employees. Bondoc said the petitioners became entitled by law and equity to benefits under the approved 2015 plan; she described the order as requiring SRA, GCG and the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) to determine, process and facilitate release of benefits due to eligible petitioners with due and deliberate dispatch. Asked about the post-separation period, she said the DA complied with Civil Service Commission requirements and would help employees; after De Jesus confirmed the August 2016 separation date, she said SRA would look into the matter and provide what was due. The exchange reports submission of names and a follow-up, not a completed eligibility determination or payment.

De Jesus asked Bondoc to confirm the FY2027 rice-production target of about 19 million metric tons and a projection that rice imports would exceed 5 million metric tons by the end of 2026; Bondoc confirmed both. Asked why imports remained high, she said importation was not DA policy, but unexpected El Nino, climate and disaster conditions, along with sharp fertilizer and diesel increases, had forced imports.

De Jesus asked whether production should shift and whether the Candaba Swamp could be used. Bondoc confirmed that the 4th District of Pampanga covers the swamp, which she said includes San Simon, San Luis, Apalit and Candaba in Pampanga and San Miguel, San Rafael and San Ildefonso in Bulacan. Her response identified the municipalities but did not set out a production-shift plan.

De Jesus cited Bureau of Customs data as of July 7, 2026: 24 criminal cases involving agricultural smuggling, 15 endorsed for prosecution under the Anti-Agricultural Economic Sabotage Act. He asked how DA identifies cases and supports their development. Bondoc said DA works on intelligence about importers and missing documents with the Philippine National Police (PNP), Bureau of Customs, Department of Justice (DOJ) and National Bureau of Investigation (NBI), and said DA still lacks enforcement capacity: its Inspectorate and Enforcement Division (DAIE) has personnel on job orders and lacks vehicles.

Bondoc described DA inspection teams at ports in Manila, the Manila International Container Port, the Ninoy Aquino International Airport, Batangas, Subic, Cebu, Davao, Zamboanga and Cagayan de Oro. She also cited DAIE teams for second inspections and condemnation activities, a Special Operations Group for high-risk operations, and an investigation branch monitoring banner programs, smuggling and economic sabotage. She said these activities continued despite limited budgets.

De Jesus put to Bondoc an estimate that strong El Nino could reduce production by about 750,000 metric tons. After clarifying that he was asking about the El Nino effect, Bondoc affirmed the figure. Asked what mitigation was planned, she cited cloud seeding, small shallow tube wells, smaller upland water-retention basins and climate-resilient crop varieties that could grow with less water. She also mentioned DA assistance and solar irrigation pumps in the exchange.

### Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago

In her round with the Department of Agriculture (DA), Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago asked which items in DA's original submission to the Department of Budget and Management (DBM), including fuel subsidies, financial assistance, and production subsidies, were not funded in the General Appropriations Bill. DA sponsor Rep. Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D. agreed that the DA budget had been reduced by 8 percent and said the department was requesting more assistance under its 11-point strategic plan to maintain production gains. She identified almost PHP 9 billion requested for expanded fertilizer assistance. She also cited farm-to-market bridge development, hybrid solar-powered ice plants, and other items she offered to share. Elago also requested a detailed report on fertilizer access, noting limited support for scaling locally developed fertilizers.

Elago opened by asking what DA programs addressed higher fuel costs, how much the department had spent in 2026, and what assistance would continue in 2027. Bondoc confirmed fuel and fertilizer assistance, then said the fuel support came from DBM savings, which she referred to as "602 to 603." She did not give a 2026 expenditure total in this exchange. In a follow-up, Elago said the PHP 2,000-plus fuel subsidy fell far short of the PHP 15,000 to PHP 30,000 a fishing boat needs for a day at sea, so some recipients used the aid for food instead of fuel. She cited the Department of Energy (DOE) certification that the US$80-per-barrel Dubai crude trigger had been reached and asked what action was planned under the emergency powers she said Congress had provided in Republic Act No. 12316. Bondoc said the DA supports in principle temporary suspension of value-added tax (VAT) and excise tax on fuel during exceptionally high prices, is adding fuel assistance to the Quick Response Fund (QRF), and is seeking more funds. Asked when a decision would come, she referred to a high-level Cabinet meeting the prior week about rising prices but gave no decision date. Elago urged prompt action and also called for consideration of removing excise taxes and the 12 percent VAT on basic needs including food, electricity, utilities, and fuel.

Elago cited the 2024 Commission on Audit (COA) Annual Audit Report observation that 58,784 farmer beneficiaries from selected regions had received Rice Farmers Financial Assistance, and that some recipients were not eligible. She asked how DA checked eligibility. Bondoc said the department reviews complaints and offered to share its list of resolved cases. She described distribution records with photographs of each farmer and said complaints about ineligible recipients could be raised at the distribution site. She said she was more concerned that eligible farmers were excluded because the Registry System for Basic Sectors in Agriculture (RSBSA) undercounts them; in her account, she hears more complaints from farmers left out than about ineligible recipients. Bondoc invited on-the-spot complaints, said DA needs to work with local government units (LGUs) because it lacks extension workers and staff to reach every farmer, and said DA would prosecute organized groups that falsely represented themselves to obtain aid. Elago said the COA observations and registration barriers still required stronger safeguards, including for small fisherfolk who also have trouble registering.

Elago cited a reported estimate of 5 million metric tons of rice imports for 2026 and the rise in rice-import dependence from 9 percent in 1990 to 23 percent in 2022. She asked whether high imports would weaken self-sufficiency and local farmers' earnings, and what DA would do beyond relying on imports. Bondoc attributed the current year's imports to El Nino, recalled that imports had fallen the prior year after suspension to support farmgate prices, and said DA remained open to interventions that would give farmers competitive prices covering input costs. She expected imports to decline the next year and hoped higher production and value for farmers would reduce import dependence.

Elago said a floor price for palay was needed after prices had fallen to PHP 5-6 per kilogram the previous year. She said she had sought reports on Executive Order No. 100, which established a floor-price mechanism, and Executive Order No. 101 on full implementation of the Sagip Saka Act, but wanted a clearer floor-price policy to protect farmers while keeping rice affordable. Bondoc said current palay pricing was adequate and described three DA approaches: higher government buying prices with the National Food Authority (NFA), which she stated as 21 for wet palay and 27 for dry; additional rented storage and 65 new warehouses; and agreements with LGUs and farmer cooperatives to use or restore underused warehouses and processing facilities. She also cited low-cost credit, market support, guaranteed buying prices, and EO 100's trigger mechanism. Elago replied that the current palay price was PHP 12-15 per kilogram and farmers were asking for a floor of at least PHP 20 per kilogram to sustain their livelihoods while maintaining access to affordable rice. She said she would follow up on DA reports and called for stronger consultation with farmers' associations.

Elago asked for the Rice Competitiveness Enhancement Fund (RCEF) allocations for 2024-2026, current rice-tariff collections, and whether the 2027 proposal included funds to cover a shortfall below the PHP 30 billion collection threshold that she said would otherwise come directly from the DA budget. She cited PHP 10 billion set aside for RCEF in 2025. Bondoc supplied tariff-collection figures, distinct from RCEF appropriation totals, of PHP 34 billion in 2024, PHP 13 billion in 2025, and PHP 4.7 billion in the first quarter of 2026. She then said there was a request for PHP 30 billion for RCEF in 2027. The answer gives the collection series and a 2027 request, but does not provide the requested 2024-2026 allocation series or specify how a 2027 tariff shortfall would be funded.

Elago said she favored moving policy away from full liberalization toward development of the local rice industry, and asked whether DA was still waiting for a version of proposed amendments from the Office of the President or economic managers. Bondoc confirmed that DA wanted to pursue several amendments to the Rice Tariffication Law. Elago requested the details and reiterated Gabriela Women's Party's call to strengthen domestic rice production, farmer and fisherfolk production, and food self-sufficiency. The sponsor did not detail the proposed amendments in this exchange.

### Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula

Representative Dadah Kiram Ismula asked about the Kabuhayan at Kaunlaran ng Kababayang Katutubo (4K) program for Indigenous peoples (IPs), including how many IP farmers and fisherfolk it had helped and its performance. Department of Agriculture (DA) sponsor Representative Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D. said that from 2022 through 2026 the program had served 256 ancestral domains and 400 IP organizations; for 2027, it planned to serve 137 domains and 196 organizations and train 2,367 Indigenous participants. Those are domain, organization, and training figures rather than an individual beneficiary count. Bondoc said participation and training were active and income rose as groups matured.

Asked for documented livelihood improvements and how they entered monitoring and evaluation, Bondoc cited buyers and market opportunities: Ueshima Coffee Company (UCC) had a 40-metric-ton coffee target involving Ati and other Mindanao tribes; SE International Manufacturing Corporation bought saba bananas from Regions 9, 10, 11, and 12; and she described ube (purple yam) activity in Regions 2, 3, 9, 10, and 11. She also cited the Mindanao Inclusive Agricultural Development Program, a World Bank loan and its first collaboration with IPs. These were reported examples; the answer did not describe a monitoring method.

Ismula asked for the 4K budget under the FY 2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA) and the proposed FY 2027 budget. Bondoc gave PHP 250 million for FY 2026 GAA and PHP 128.29 million for FY 2027 NEP. She said all aspects would be reduced, but DA would try to continue assistance by integrating 4K participants into DA banner programs. Ismula voiced support for continuing and strengthening the program and asked that funding intended for Indigenous communities be restored as an appeal for continued support.

Ismula asked for an update on the weather-index parametric insurance piloted by DA and the Bureau of Fisheries and Aquatic Resources (BFAR) with the Philippine Crop Insurance Corporation (PCIC) for registered municipal fisherfolk, which pays a pre-agreed amount when specified triggers such as high wind speeds or a typhoon signal occur. Bondoc said the pilot had covered 14,215 fisherfolk in Occidental Mindoro, Antique, Cebu, Surigao del Norte, and Negros Oriental, with a PHP 10 million budget for 2025-2026, and that an actuarial model was being developed.

Ismula said local government units in Negros Oriental and Surigao del Norte had endorsed continuation and asked whether phase two was in the FY 2027 budget. Bondoc confirmed inclusion; Ismula stated that phase two needed PHP 10 million for the target area, and Bondoc confirmed that amount.

Ismula relayed a colleague's question about Fisheries Administrative Order 266, which Ismula said the Supreme Court sitting en banc declared unconstitutional on January 21, 2026, invalidating mandatory Vessel Monitoring System (VMS) and Electronic Reporting System (ERS) requirements for commercial Philippine-flag vessels. Bondoc said BFAR still implements vessel monitoring where required by international fisheries obligations, but acknowledged that enforcement capacity had weakened. She listed alternative tools including monitoring, control and surveillance (MCS) patrol boats, physical inspections, sea patrols, onboard observers, logbook audits, port controls, licensing, and intelligence gathering.

Asked how many unregistered commercial vessels BFAR had identified, Bondoc answered that it had recorded almost 2,500 registered vessels. When Ismula asked how many had been apprehended and about case status, Bondoc gave 158 for 2025 and 76 for 2026 without clarifying the count type or case outcomes.

Asked about VMS/ERS spending, Bondoc gave a total of PHP 3.440 billion from 2018 through 2026. She said that as of June 30, 2026, PHP 40 million remained for VMS and that the FY 2027 proposed budget included PHP 500 million for its operationalization and sustainability.

Ismula compared the DA total of PHP 186 billion in the FY 2026 GAA with PHP 171 billion in the FY 2027 NEP, describing a PHP 15 billion decrease; Ismula noted that the Department of Budget and Management often attributes departmental cuts to weak absorptive capacity. For the Production Support Services subprogram, Ismula cited PHP 35.6 billion in the FY 2026 GAA, PHP 29.8 billion in the FY 2027 NEP, and a PHP 5.8 billion cut. Asked for the original proposal to DBM for that subprogram, Bondoc said DA proposed PHP 42 billion for FY 2027, of which PHP 29.7 billion appeared in the NEP. The two speakers gave slightly different FY 2027 NEP amounts for the subprogram; the exchange does not reconcile them.

Asked about effects on production capacity and farmer income, Bondoc said reduced hybrid-seed coverage was nearly balanced by expanded inbred-seed support under RCEF and that DA had raised the hybrid-rice seedling rate. Because the overall National Rice Program budget ceiling had not increased, she said the added seed support needed to be matched with fertilizer funding and requested a larger fertilizer allocation. Ismula then asked about the effect of lower support on food prices, citing 5.2% food inflation in July 2026. Bondoc said rice was a major component of inflation, hoped lower budget support would not increase inflation, and urged colleagues to support and, if possible, increase DA funding. Ismula closed by urging congressional support for farmers, fisherfolk, and food producers and arguing that reducing support would threaten producer livelihoods and raise food prices.

### Rep. Antonio L. Tinio

Rep. Antonio L. Tinio asked about fertilizer availability and prices ahead of the next planting season. Sponsor Rep. Anna York P. Bondoc, M.D., said fertilizer imports continued at higher prices and that the Department of Agriculture (DA) was seeking more assistance for farmers. Asked the price of a bag and the increase, she gave a range of 2.3 to 2.6 per bag; her answer did not state a currency or denomination.

Bondoc identified PHP 2.2 billion as 2026 additional fertilizer funding, describing it in different answers as for foliar and biofertilizer or organic fertilizers. When Tinio asked if it was for chemical or inorganic fertilizer, she first agreed; when he asked specifically about biofertilizers, she said the PHP 2.2 billion was for organic fertilizers. Asked its funding source, she said it came from the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) through Uplift; she agreed with Tinio's characterization of it as an augmentation. She also said no amount had originally been appropriated for biofertilizers in 2026. In a later utilization answer she said the funding had just been released and was part of the 2026 GAE, but also said it was not part of the PHP 2.2 billion, leaving that relationship unresolved.

For FY2027, Bondoc said the RCEF fund included PHP 1.8 billion for biofertilizers. When asked for 2025 procurement and utilization information, she later gave a 2025 amount of PHP 1.8 billion; the exchange did not establish a release or utilization status for that amount.

Tinio asked what standards DA would use to select biofertilizer suppliers, citing a list of 61 registered products from the Fertilizer and Pesticide Authority (FPA). Bondoc first named Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approval and a field-rice derby; after Tinio raised FPA registration, she confirmed products must be FPA-registered, field-tested and supported by a score sheet, and confirmed PhilRice benchmarking. She described head-to-head field tests against standards and spot inspections, and agreed that field-rice benchmarking remained a requirement.

Because 2026 procurement was not yet underway, Tinio requested 2025 supplier and procurement data, including whether products met FPA registration or PhilRice benchmarking, citing the scale of spending and the fertilizer-funds scam history as reasons for scrutiny. Bondoc agreed to provide the complete report. On the 2026 augmentation, she said the SARO had been issued only a few weeks earlier and bidding was still scheduled; Tinio confirmed there had been no procurement yet. The exchange records the commitment and pending bidding, not an awarded contract.

Tinio turned to the proposed FY2027 farm-to-market road program, stating a PHP 16 billion proposal across 703 projects, most at PHP 30 million or less. He highlighted higher-cost projects, including a PHP 500 million single project and a PHP 250 million project; in the Cordillera Administrative Region (CAR), he said the total was PHP 1.54 billion, of which PHP 1.03 billion covered eight projects in Benguet. He also pointed to PHP 2.5 billion for Sultan Kudarat in 2026.

Bondoc said CAR allocations followed specific roadmaps and were intended to front-load a road network for high-value crops, logistics and later production support; smaller sums could build only short road segments and leave the network inadequate. Tinio asked whether a PHP 500 million project could realistically be implemented in one fiscal year. Referring to documents DA-CAR supplied at the budget briefing, he said the project covered 33 kilometers but only about 9 kilometers had been validated; he later cited the document as showing 8-point-something of 33 kilometers. Bondoc said the project was fully validated. Tinio disagreed, relying on the DA-CAR document.

Bondoc said road delivery depended on clear right-of-way and land-tenure issues, and that delays also came from late SARO releases. She said she would obtain and share validation details. Tinio said his concern was whether funds would actually be used rather than tied up in a project that might be only partly implemented; the validation disagreement was not resolved in this exchange.

Tinio cited DA's FMR Watch transparency portal for 2021-2026: 6,428 projects and PHP 109.5 billion in appropriated funds, with 3,235 projects marked complete. He also cited a 44% completion rate and 2,550 projects not yet started, including 11 from 2021, 147 from 2023, 320 from 2024 and 279 from 2025; he said most or all 2026 projects had not started. The portal figures were Tinio's presentation of available data, and the reported completion count and percentage were not reconciled in the exchange.

Asked whether DA confirmed the portal data, Bondoc first said DA would check the portal and asked to focus on 2025. She later clarified that the portal data came from the Department of Public Works and Highways (DPWH), which she agreed was the implementing agency, and said DA was validating it against its own data and could not speak for DPWH's figures. Tinio replied that the portal remained the available record and reiterated the implementation delays shown there.

On FY2025 farm-to-market roads, Bondoc said PHP 23 billion was allocated, PHP 13 billion released, and 75% implemented as of August. She said PHP 10.7 billion had been under For Later Release (FLR) and was released the previous Friday. She later described 46% of the budget as FLR; Tinio summarized PHP 10.7 billion out of PHP 23 billion as about 40%.

For 2026, Tinio cited 1,699 FMR projects with PHP 34.2 billion in appropriations and asked what share was FLR. Bondoc said DBM had released a PHP 4.7 billion SARO in August and that an omnibus SARO request for PHP 18 billion, submitted in June, was still awaiting release. She said DA did not know whether the pending amount would be FLR, while the Secretary had been assured there would be no FLRs that year; Tinio interjected that there would be. The exchange did not produce an exact 2026 FLR percentage.

Asked why releases and bidding were delayed, Bondoc said DA was not responsible for FLR decisions and suggested asking the responsible department. Tinio argued that DBM would attribute delays to congressional amendments or insertions requiring complete documents and a validation process that could consume most of the fiscal year and postpone SARO release until near year-end. He said similar FLR delays affected DPWH capital-outlay programs, that projects could take at least two years to complete, and that late implementation delayed services and tied up funds that might be used more urgently elsewhere. He distinguished this service-delivery concern from a separate corruption question.

### Termination of deliberations

After the Majority Leader moved to recognize him, Representative Elijah Rumbaoa San Fernando said no minority member remained to interpellate on the FY 2027 Department of Agriculture (DA) budget and moved to terminate sponsorship and debate on the DA budget and its attached agencies. Eduardo R. Rama Jr. said the majority joined the motion. Presiding Deputy Speaker Yevgeny Vincente B. Emano put the joint motion to the House, heard no objection, and declared the DA budget interpellations terminated. In a later clarification, Emano amended the ruling to include the DA's attached agencies and corporations; again hearing no objection, he reiterated that deliberations were terminated.

[07:23:15 · asr-007100](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007100) · [07:23:24 · asr-007104](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007104) · [07:23:32 · asr-007109](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007109) · [07:23:58 · asr-007120](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007120) · [07:30:37 · asr-007150](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007150)

## Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG)

### DILG opening budget presentation

Sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella urged support for DILG's additional request for 1,095 emergency response vehicles, reinstatement of the Seal of Good Local Governance Initiative, and peace-and-order measures including stronger Lupong Tagapamayapa.

For the National Police Commission (NAPOLCOM), he sought additional support for independence and police oversight, securing and modernizing sensitive records, and a special provision to use self-generated income for repairs, maintenance, equipment, facilities, and personnel benefits. For the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP), he sought 2,000 additional fire officers and a special provision permitting use of at least 80 percent of annual fee collections, which he described as PHP 4.5 billion, for capability gaps. He cited 74 search-and-rescue trucks and 82 ambulances. The fee collections were described as available for BFP use; the special provision is a revenue-use request, not another NEP amount.

For the Philippine National Police (PNP), he sought additional support for a regional headquarters in the Negros Island Region, completion of the Region 1 regional-office facility, stronger tourist-police units, 23 police stations and buildings, and a PNP Counterterrorism Center. The presentation urged support for these items; it records no vote or disposition.

Fuentebella framed the budget as funding government protection through barangay, city, municipal, provincial, and national services. He cited a 96.33 percent DILG emergency-response efficiency rate and five-to-seven-minute response times, along with 50,441 evacuation centers and more than 126,000 uniformed personnel deployed for disaster response. He said DILG had strengthened school-security protocols and safer school zones, working with the Department of Education and police-community partnerships.

He said NAPOLCOM's Zero Case Backlog Initiative resolved 2,404 administrative cases involving 4,084 PNP personnel, clearing cases accumulated over 25 years. He reported BFP fire incidents down 27.23 percent, operation of a unified 911 system, and activated fire stations and trucks in 92 percent of municipalities.

For the PNP, he reported declines in focused crimes (12.4 percent), indexed and non-indexed crimes (2.37 percent), and cybercrime cases (30 percent), with crime-clearance efficiency at 98 percent and solution efficiency at 90 percent. Over the past year, the PNP conducted 55,843 operations, arrested 67,223 people, and seized illegal drugs reported as worth approximately 25.3 billion. He also said the Bureau of Jail Management and Penology had reduced jail congestion and intensified inspections that confiscated illicit cash, drugs, electronic devices, and weapons.

Fuentebella said DILG continued to support and monitor more than 3,000 local-government support-fund projects benefiting about 34 million households. It trained barangay, Sangguniang Kabataan, and newly elected officials and advanced digitalization, with more than 1,202 local-government units using automated systems. Through the Early Childhood Care and Development Council, 91.68 percent of barangays had child-development centers. He credited the Philippine Commission on Women with the country's gender-parity standing in Asia, the National Commission on Muslim Filipinos with supporting safe Hajj pilgrimages, and the Local Government Academy and Philippine Public Safety College with training local and uniformed officials. He closed by linking local capability, accountable institutions, and community safety.

### Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula

Rep. Dadah Kiram Ismula said she would not question the DILG sponsor, but used her time to support increased budgets for the National Commission on Muslim Filipinos (NCMF) and the Philippine Commission on Women (PCW). Citing NCMF's mandate under Republic Act No. 9997, she described its work to preserve and develop Muslim Filipinos' culture, traditions, institutions, and well-being, including administering the annual Hajj to Mecca. She said funding was insufficient, particularly for the medical team caring for pilgrims, and identified madrasa rehabilitation and halal programs as essential needs.

For PCW, she cited its work on gender-responsive policies, advocacy, technical assistance, and monitoring gender mainstreaming in the bureaucracy. She also referred to a long-standing funding shortfall for regional offices without assigning that concern to one agency. Ismula expressed full support for budget increases for both agencies and said she believed their budgets would receive favorable consideration under the sponsor's guidance. She linked her support to honest spending and fulfillment of the agencies' mandates. No amount or adopted increase was recorded.

### Rep. Presley C. De Jesus

In his DILG round, Rep. Presley C. De Jesus described the department's proposed expenditure program as rising from PHP 310 billion in 2026 to PHP 332 billion in 2027. He said personal services accounted for PHP 21.50 billion, or about 97.6 percent of the net increase, while capital outlays declined by about PHP 1.97 billion. The round does not state an original DBM request or a prior-year GAA comparison.

DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella attributed the increases to personal services. On capital outlays, he asked De Jesus to narrow the question across DILG and its attached agencies, then said many fire and police stations had been built but acknowledged fire stations were still insufficient. De Jesus referred to capital outlays previously being around PHP 21 billion, but qualified that recollection as approximate and did not receive a more specific account of the reduction's causes or procurement effect.

Fuentebella first itemized PHP 4.3 billion for retirement gratuity, PHP 6.4 billion for terminal leave, and PHP 2.091 billion to create 6,000 positions. When De Jesus followed up on compensation and benefits versus new personnel, Fuentebella repeated the PHP 4.3 billion and PHP 6.4 billion figures but put new positions at PHP 2.9 billion. The round leaves the two recruitment-cost figures unreconciled; it does not attribute a separate amount to compensation adjustments.

De Jesus asked how lower capital outlays would affect equipment and construction. Fuentebella said the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) had built many fire stations but still needed more, and asked colleagues to help secure funding. He said the BFP collected PHP 4.5 billion a year, while the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) approved PHP 1 billion per year, and sought 80 percent of that year's collections by 2027 to address shortages in fire trucks and stations. He linked the request to a department effort to clean up the BFP, move examinations online and study government-to-government procurement with South Korea for greater transparency and accountability. No additional NEP appropriation or formal plenary motion was recorded in this exchange.

De Jesus said the proposed Local Government Empowerment Program allocation was PHP 6.494 billion and its 2025 disbursement rate was 69 percent. Fuentebella identified late release of additional funds through SARO, an election ban that allowed only ongoing projects to continue, and delays in obtaining required national-agency certificates. He also cited newly elected local officials needing training. Fuentebella asked whether the project had happened, and De Jesus answered yes.

On the Early Childhood Care and Development (ECCD) Council, De Jesus compared a proposed expenditure program of PHP 357 million with PHP 124.2 million, an approximately 65 percent reduction, and asked about effects on local services. Fuentebella cited a June 2026 obligation rate of 45.56 percent, then a second June 2026 rate of 94.58 percent without clearly naming its measure; he compared these with a 2025 obligation rate of 92.09 percent and a 2025 disbursement rate of 72.7 percent. He said the new law removed establishment of NCDCs and thus infrastructure, while the Council's continuing work focused on quality care and education and coordination with local governments on integrated health and nutrition. He believed local governments, barangays or Congress could help construct facilities. He also said the mandate applied in remote areas and offered to accompany the member to places not being reached.

De Jesus asked what DILG had identified as needing improvement after reported school shootings and concerns about minors' firearm access, warning reports and coordination among schools, local governments and law enforcement. Fuentebella declined to disclose case specifics during an ongoing investigation. He said DILG had coordinated with the Department of Education (DepEd), Department of Information and Communications Technology (DICT) and Department of Social Welfare and Development (DSWD), and cited DILG Memorandum Circular 2026.037 on safer school zones beyond school gates, including surrounding roads, sidewalks, crossings, waiting and transport areas. Barangays were to lead local implementation, and police were training barangay tanods. He described online sites as a concern and called for stronger platform regulation; De Jesus added that his earlier DICT budget deferment also concerned the department's performance, not only Meta.

Fuentebella said police intelligence reports had helped save more than 40 children, but he could not disclose the reports' source. On reporting, he pointed to 911 and estimated police could arrive in five to seven minutes. He said prevention needed community participation by parents, PTAs, teachers, barangay and local-government officials, and called for police and DILG capability, including a counterterrorism building he said was still being sought. He said the issue required an interagency composite team and that police could not address it alone.

[08:06:01 · asr-007953](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-007953)

### Rep. Roberto Nazal

Rep. Roberto Nazal asked how many local government units (LGUs) provide the PHP 1,000 monthly subsidy for qualified solo parents and how many parents receive it, citing complaints that the subsidy and solo-parent booklet are not reaching beneficiaries. DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella first clarified that implementation involves a joint circular with the Department of Social Welfare and Development, then agreed DILG should monitor LGU implementation. He did not provide the requested counts and asked to submit the data; Nazal accepted submission.

Nazal also followed up on DILG's earlier promise to study including compliance with the Solo Parents' Welfare Act in the Seal of Good Local Governance. Fuentebella said the work had moved to implementation in a technical working group, expected new regulations to be released within about a month, and placed implementation in January of the following year. Nazal requested quarterly LGU-compliance status reports to help follow up noncompliant LGUs; Fuentebella noted the request.

Nazal asked why the 9-1-1 Emergency Services Program's 2025 obligation rate was 2.45%, what had not been implemented and what had changed. Fuentebella attributed the problem to vehicles and said they had been updated. Asked to distinguish appropriations, releases, obligations and disbursements for 2025 and 2026, Fuentebella first said the 2025 amount was PHP 1 billion, then, after a request to clarify 2026, said disbursement was around PHP 1.4 billion for 2025 and referred to both 2025 and 2026. The answers do not reconcile those figures or their fiscal-year and spending-status scope.

Nazal requested a breakdown across the technology platform, private service providers, command centers, telecommunications, ambulances and other vehicles, personnel, and training. Fuentebella asked for time to submit it, and Nazal asked that it be provided that day. This is program spending, not DILG's overall NEP allocation or original agency request.

Nazal asked for genuine emergencies resulting in dispatch, arrival-time response, successful assistance or stabilization, resolved emergencies and lives saved. Fuentebella gave an average response time of five to seven minutes, said the outcome data could be pulled and submitted to Nazal, and cited National Call Center figures for September 11, 2025 through July 31, 2026: 14,461,871 incoming calls, 13,931,388 handled calls, 96.33% handling efficiency, 513,483 abandoned calls and 17,469 documented prank calls, with approximately 53,000 incoming calls per day. When Nazal asked how many actual emergency lives were saved, Fuentebella repeated the handled-call figures rather than giving an emergency-outcome count.

Nazal proposed adding measurable emergency outcomes as a 9-1-1 performance indicator. Fuentebella agreed to include the suggestion. The call totals and handling rate describe call-center activity; the requested emergency outcomes remained for follow-up.

Nazal argued that timely arrival alone is not enough if an ambulance lacks someone trained to begin lifesaving care. He asked whether the 9-1-1 budget could train LGU personnel through existing Technical Education and Skills Development Authority (TESDA) programs, and proposed a national standard requiring a trained, certified paramedic on each LGU or 9-1-1 ambulance, with a reasonable transition period. Fuentebella said DILG was already linking training, cited 5,000 nurses under BFP and said they were qualified, and agreed that DILG would work with TESDA and the Department of Health on a standard; he also said DILG was working on it. He initially noted that a binding requirement might need legislation because DILG supervises LGUs.

Asked whether the 2027 9-1-1 budget could start the training or what authority Congress should provide, Fuentebella pointed to BFP personnel's NC2 training and said the work should continue despite funding limits, but did not directly answer the budget or authority question. He described coordination across DILG, BFP and LGUs, while Nazal reiterated the potential for pre-hospital assessment and stabilization. Fuentebella said BFP personnel extend training to local responders and agreed to work toward the standard and transition.

Nazal asked whether eligible persons deprived of liberty (PDLs) were registered with PhilHealth and whether Bureau of Jail Management and Penology (BJMP) facilities linked them to Yakap-accredited providers and covered medicines through Yakap Gamot. Fuentebella said not all facilities were connected but implementation had begun; he offered to provide the facility count that day. On coordination with PhilHealth, he said BJMP was already using the program for covered medicines, while Nazal asked BJMP to improve registration and qualification and Fuentebella said the work was ongoing. The requested facility count was not supplied in this exchange.

Nazal cited a House budget-research review of Commission on Audit (COA) findings as of end-2025: 100 municipalities had no activated fire station, 64 had neither a fire station nor a fire truck, and BFP shortages included 20,854 fire coats, 22,019 gloves and 36,888 fireman's hoods. He asked for a national master plan identifying each LGU or equipment gap, needs and cost, funding source including earmarked revenue, and the year each deficiency would be closed; he also asked for a scientific basis for station and truck numbers and replacement timing. Fuentebella agreed BFP faced major challenges and said its own current counts were 61 missing fire stations and 106 fire trucks. He said planning was becoming data-driven, with 40-year-old trucks prioritized; the stated lifespan was up to 20 years. These were distinct figures and descriptions from the member's cited review and the sponsor's response.

Fuentebella said BFP's needs were not only addressed through the GAA and that the department was seeking release of 80% of fees earmarked for BFP. He described data-based planning, government-to-government fire-truck procurement and prioritizing equipment to local need, and said the agency needed release of the funds. Nazal supported release of earmarked funds, including for BFP, and praised the Eurofire government-to-government procurement.

Fuentebella said BFP lacked 40,000 personnel and that DBM was providing 2,000 personnel per year, which he said would take 20 years to fill the gap. He said they were seeking an additional 2,000 personnel per year for ten years, citing fire suppression, rescue and 9-1-1 duties as well as the need to improve service. Nazal then asked BFP to submit a national master plan with annual targets to Congress. Fuentebella agreed and proposed submission by Monday of the following week. This was a request and commitment in the debate, not a recorded appropriation motion or approval.

[08:35:31 · asr-008468](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-008468)

### Rep. Iris Marie Demesa Montes

Rep. Iris Marie Demesa Montes asked the Philippine Commission on Women (PCW) for the total estimated 5% Gender and Development (GAD) budget across government entities for FY2027. Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella said the data, including for local government units (LGUs), was not then available and promised to obtain it. After Montes narrowed the request to national agencies, Fuentebella cited PHP 1 trillion for 2025, a separate year from the requested FY2027 estimate; the exchange clarified that LGUs were outside that figure. The requested FY2027 total was not supplied.

Montes then asked for compliance rates on GAD plans, budgets, and accomplishment reports, separated between LGUs and national entities. Fuentebella said LGU information would need to be obtained from DILG and municipalities and that PCW lacked capacity to monitor LGUs. For national agencies, government-owned or controlled corporations (GOCCs), and state universities and colleges (SUCs), he gave a 77% compliance rate. Asked which agencies were noncompliant, he said they could be named and had been reported to the Commission on Human Rights (CHR), but he did not have the record then; he offered to submit the names by about 6:30 that day. The record here shows the offer, not delivery.

Montes described implementation gaps under the Magna Carta of Women and recalled seeking an investigation into GAD-budget utilization in a privileged speech the previous year. She asked what amendments PCW proposed to move from a spending target to a legally enforceable, outcome-based GAD framework. Fuentebella said PCW had 18 bills and offered to submit them; he did not identify enactment or disposition.

Montes also asked whether Congress should add a General Appropriations Act (GAA) provision to prevent paper compliance and require proper use of GAD funds. Fuentebella cited existing administrative penalties and the Commission on Audit (COA) monitoring GAD expenditures. Asked about a mandatory joint COA-PCW audit and performance reviews before GAD funds were used, he said that audit fell outside the PCW mandate, which rests with COA, though PCW could help supervise. Montes framed her questions as seeking a forward-looking policy response under current law, and Fuentebella expressed support for her aims. No special provision or audit framework was adopted in this exchange.

[08:44:10 · asr-008706](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-008706) · [08:44:25 · asr-008713](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-008713)

### Rep. Edgar R. Erice

Rep. Edgar R. Erice cited Local Government Support Fund (LGSF) allocations of PHP 27 billion in 2025 and PHP 57 billion in 2026, with a proposed PHP 58 billion for 2027. He recalled an earlier LGSF of about PHP 410 million in 2014, which he said had been challenged in the Supreme Court; he described the Court's rationale as resting on a singular implementation and definite recipients. Erice said DILG subsequently made the fund a line item and set parameters around necessity and prioritization. DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella said criteria still applied through the portal, while the eligible uses had broadened.

Erice asked what DILG did with its general-supervision role as LGSF grew. Fuentebella said DILG monitored and evaluated projects, provided technical assistance and coordination, and supervised implementation after a grant; DBM released the funds. He said that when DILG found project errors, it informed the LGU and referred matters to the Commission on Audit (COA). At the time of the exchange, he said DILG had found no such implementation error, while acknowledging that it did not have complete LGSF allocation data.

Erice asked whether DILG could reject applications that were not priorities in a local development plan, were not project-ready, duplicated other funding, or came from LGUs with weak absorptive capacity or pending audit findings. Fuentebella said DILG had no authority to approve or disapprove applications. He described a coordination process involving DBM, DILG, and COA, with criteria including consistency with the local development plan and checks against duplicate projects or funding. Applications went through the DBM portal; DBM reviewed and selected qualified LGUs and released funds directly. Fuentebella said all LGUs were eligible to apply and that a scorecard existed, but referred questions on its operation to DBM. He also said unfinished projects could affect later releases under DBM guidelines.

Erice argued that the LGSF's scale made equitable allocation and oversight important. He compared Quezon City, which he said had more than PHP 40 billion in combined Internal Revenue Allotment, national-government, and local revenues and received almost PHP 1 billion from LGSF in 2026, with smaller or poorer localities for which he cited LGSF examples of PHP 10 million, PHP 13 million, and PHP 19 million. He asked whether those distributions were equitable. Fuentebella agreed that fairness mattered but said he needed data; he could not assess equity and said DILG had no complete data on LGSF amounts distributed to municipalities. He said all LGUs received LGSF in 2026 and acknowledged the policy could improve. Erice urged DILG to develop a stronger policy role; Fuentebella said the issue was ongoing discussion among government bodies and Congress could use its oversight function.

In response to Erice's call for corruption detection and enforcement, Fuentebella cited about 684 additional technical-support staff hired in 2026, the public Subaybayan portal for project progress, third-party project monitoring, and a requirement that projects be in an LGU comprehensive development plan before application through the DBM portal. He said DILG field officers, engineers, and project-evaluation staff monitored implementation. Erice's proposal for a stronger DILG oversight policy remained a question and was not adopted in this exchange.

### Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc

Rep. Sergio C. Dagooc asked whether the Department of the Interior and Local Government (DILG) knew of local government units (LGUs) continuing to assess or collect taxes on electric cooperatives under Joint Memorandum Circular 2024-12-001, issued by the Department of Finance (DOF) and Department of Energy (DOE). He said some LGUs continued assessing the taxes despite the circular and that electric cooperatives passed them on to consumers. DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella pointed to DILG Memorandum Circular 2025-041. He said the circular was a memorandum, but DILG could investigate identified errors or noncompliance; for faster action, Dagooc could sue the LGU.

Dagooc said he had identified about four LGUs at an earlier budget briefing and asked whether DILG officers in each LGU could be authorized to address noncompliance. Fuentebella said DILG would obtain data from LGUs and identify them, then replied that the LGU had committed to Dagooc's suggestion. The response records a commitment, but does not name the LGUs or specify the field officers' authority.

Dagooc said that after deliberation only nontechnical system losses could be removed, while technical losses would remain but could be reduced. He linked the issue to Section 37F and Section 10 of Republic Act 7832, the Anti-Electricity Pilferage Act, and characterized Section 10 as vague. He argued that enforcement should involve police rather than barangay officials and asked DILG to help develop a proposal to amend the law.

Fuentebella said DILG had issued a measure in 2006 and would reissue it to help with anti-pilferage enforcement. On amendments focused on enforcement, he said the matter would be studied further and that statutory amendments were for Congress and the Senate. Dagooc reiterated a proposed amendment addressing removal of nontechnical system loss, and Fuentebella agreed. The exchange records agreement and a reissue/study commitment, not a formal motion or enacted amendment.

[09:15:21 · asr-009918](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-009918) · [09:15:38 · asr-009919](https://tordecilla.github.io/budget-hearings-2026/records/72/#asr-009919)

### Rep. Percival "Perci" Cendaña

Cendaña asked for a DILG update after hearing of Harry Roque's asylum request denial. DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella said the matter was legal, described the ordinary process as six months, and said that period had passed without approval or disapproval; he also said the Philippine Center on Transnational Crime had submitted the documents Interpol requested for a red notice. Asked for a timeline for Roque's return, the transcript attributes the statement "We will have to follow up with the Interpol" to Cendaña. No return date or further disposition was given.

Cendaña said Unified 9-1-1 had gone live in the National Capital Region, BARMM, Region I and Region VII, while PNP had reported that reliability depended on mobile signal strength. He asked whether calls in weak-signal areas would receive no response and whether DILG planned redundancy or offline service. Fuentebella said DILG was coordinating with the Department of Information and Communications Technology (DICT), including its last-mile effort, to avoid duplication; he cited Starlink distribution to schools and said government intervention could fill gaps, particularly in coastal areas where telecommunications companies had not entered. Asked which budget would fund last-mile investment, he said it was principally for private telecommunications companies, with government intervening where needed.

Cendaña raised a reported August delay in emergency response to a senior citizen in Taguig, who later died. Fuentebella asked for specifics so he could answer and act appropriately; Cendaña said he would forward the incident details. On the broader dispatch-to-medical-response coordination question, Fuentebella said 9-1-1 calls were recorded and the data could show what happened in the Taguig case once details were provided. The unit records no case-specific follow-up result.

Asked how many calls 9-1-1 had received and handled, Fuentebella cited 14,461,871 incoming calls, 13,931,388 handled calls, 96.33% handling efficiency, 530,483 abandoned calls, 17,469 documented prank calls and about 53,000 incoming calls per day. He said the recorded calls would allow DILG to establish what happened in the Taguig case once Cendaña supplied the specific details. These call-handling counts were not presented as counts of emergencies resolved, patients stabilized or lives saved.

Asked what FY2027 funding was allocated for emergency-response vehicles and 9-1-1 command-center infrastructure, Fuentebella said PHP 224 million. Cendaña said this seemed small for the country and asked how many more regions the current General Appropriations Bill allocation could cover. Fuentebella said ten satellite command centers served all regions and were language-sensitive. Asked what further amount was needed for robust systems, he first said PHP 372 million was needed, then stated PHP 373 million. He described PHP 373 million as additional FY2027 operating costs for 9-1-1, and clarified that the ambulance request was separate. Cendaña offered support for the additional funds and cited his filed House Bill 8417 to institutionalize an emergency medical services system. The exchange does not reconcile PHP 372 million with PHP 373 million or state the amount of the separate ambulance request.

Cendaña described existing requirements for Sangguniang Kabataan (SK) councils to post their youth development plans, annual investment programs, resolutions and receipts-and-expenditures statements on the physical SK Full Disclosure Policy Board; online posting was encouraged but not mandatory. Asked what share of barangays posted SK budgets, youth plans or quarterly financial statements online, Fuentebella said he had no figure and would look into it. Cendaña asked whether DILG and the National Youth Commission could make social-media posting mandatory. Fuentebella agreed and committed to do so; Cendaña urged prompt implementation for transparency and accountability.

Cendaña raised a school shooting in which three children died and eight were injured, noting that the firearm was government-issued and that an initial police report suggested children may have been influenced by online true-crime communities. Fuentebella said legislation should strengthen firearm safekeeping requirements, including for licensed holders, and described the issue as also involving social media and broader social conditions.

Cendaña asked whether DILG and PNP would conduct a comprehensive safety audit and physical accounting of government-issued firearms. Fuentebella said PNP was already doing this. Asked about reviewing rules for possession, authorization, firearm condition and safekeeping, he agreed, then broadened his response from government-issued weapons to all firearms. Cendaña said he planned a resolution urging a safety audit of government firearms assigned to personnel and asked that DILG and PNP prioritize the concern. He also cited filed House Bill 158 on child safety against gun violence.

Cendaña asked whether DILG and PNP, with the Department of Education (DepEd), would study transparent school bags. Fuentebella said this was a DepEd policy and that DILG was working with DepEd, the Department of Social Welfare and Development and DICT. Cendaña noted the possible added cost for students and said a transparent bag could make it harder to conceal a firearm or knife; he cited House Bill 158 and his planned resolution on firearms audits.

In closing, Fuentebella listed police assistance during school opening and closing, public-safety operations and police visibility under Oplan Balik Eskwela for School Year 2026-2027, and training barangay tanods with LGU coordination to extend community protection. Cendaña called for a whole-of-government and whole-of-nation approach to safety in schools and communities.

### Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago

Rep. Sarah Jane I. Elago raised the Early Childhood Care and Development Council (ECCD Council)'s reported 65 percent budget cut. She identified a PHP 20 million reduction in a program supporting public and private child development centers, and a PHP 182 million reduction for accrediting ECCD service providers, from PHP 207 million to about PHP 24 million.

DILG sponsor Rep. Arnie B. Fuentebella said the Council had requested PHP 1 billion; the FY2027 National Expenditure Program (NEP) included PHP 124 million, compared with PHP 357 million in the FY2026 General Appropriations Act (GAA). He urged colleagues to increase funding and also cited a PHP 901,716,000 request. The exchange does not establish whether that request is the same as, a component of, or separate from the PHP 1 billion request. When Elago asked why the Department of Budget and Management (DBM) reduced the NEP amount, Fuentebella said DBM was not present and no reason had been given to them; Elago asked the Council to provide a clearer explanation.

Elago asked whether DILG and the Council supported restoring the prior level for capacity building and institutional development. Fuentebella said yes. This was support for restoration, not an appropriation vote or a stated approved amount.

Elago said the Philippine Commission on Women (PCW) faced a 13 percent budget cut, especially for planning, management and monitoring of gender-mainstreaming rules under the Magna Carta of Women, as well as reductions in technical assistance, advisory services and gender-and-development capacity building. She called for restoring these programs to their FY2026 level or adding funds, alongside support for the National Youth Commission (NYC) and other policy-making bodies. The round records no sponsor response or disposition on this PCW request before Elago moved to public-market fees.

Elago asked where the reported 8.32 percent of barangays without a child development center were located and when the status of centers was last validated. Fuentebella said some barangays share centers where there is no buildable space and described facilities and training as priorities in his district. He offered region-level data; he then stated that, out of 42,000 barangays, 75,880 had existing centers. That count does not reconcile with the denominator or with the 91.68 percent coverage figure Elago cited, and the source does not clarify its basis.

Elago requested city/province and center details, including numbers of child development workers and teachers, for submission to her representation and the House. She noted that in some third-, fourth- and fifth-class municipalities, centers existed but still lacked complete facilities, staff and equipment to provide safe spaces. She asked DILG and the ECCD Council to report on monitoring and evaluation. Fuentebella agreed to submit the information.

Elago asked Fuentebella to explain the country's Global Gender Gap Index parity score. He described education, health and political participation as parameters and offered to submit a report released the prior week. Elago said the index reflects how gender affects access to rights, resources and opportunities, not the quality, accessibility or availability of services. She cited scores of 0.78 for economic participation and opportunity, 0.98 for educational attainment, 0.98 for health and survival, and 0.37 for political empowerment; she explained the last as 37 percent of a 100 percent equal-rights and representation goal.

Citing a final Gender and Development (GAD) utilization report from the Department of Budget and Management (DBM), which she said came from PCW, Elago said 80.62 percent of allocated agency GAD budgets had been used. She named the Department of Labor and Employment (DOLE) as the leading national agency in GAD allocation and utilization, citing a PHP 36 billion total agency budget and PHP 30.2 billion GAD allocation (83 percent of its budget). Fuentebella said the figures were reviewed and had means of verification. Elago also cited the Philippine Army as fifth-ranked with 15.37 percent; the measure for that figure was not specified in this exchange.

Elago asked whether PCW had endorsed a Men's Rights Seminar in the Department of National Defense (DND) GAD plan and budget. Fuentebella initially appeared to confirm, then said he did not recall whether PCW had endorsed that particular seminar, asked for its date and details so they could verify, and said PCW supported equal rights for women and men. Elago said GAD covers equal rights and access for women, men and gender-diverse people, and called for review of each activity to ensure it advanced equality rather than backlash. The specific endorsement remained unconfirmed in this round.

Elago reported that vendors consulted at San Andres Market said daily rent had risen from PHP 60 in 2016 to PHP 169. At Santa Ana Market, she said daily rent had risen from PHP 35 to PHP 120. She also listed accumulated business-permit, fire-safety, health, utilities, garbage, developer and annual contract-renewal fees, and asked how DILG monitored local charges and ensured local governments did not exceed their powers.

Fuentebella said DILG's role was general supervision, while respecting local autonomy. He described the process as requiring a tax ordinance and public consultation and said DILG's authority was limited to supervision when the process was followed and charges were not abusive. Elago then asked what monitoring mechanism and redress were available. No answer to Elago's final question is recorded here; her discussion of market oversight and vendor protections continues in Part 2.
